) which only accounts for the leading order contribution to
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
from an incorrectly matched anisotropic distribution function
f_{a}
. Inclusion of the residual shear stress from
\delta\tilde{f}
modifies the energy conservation law as follows:

\partial_{\rho}\hat{I}_{200}(\hat{\Lambda},\xi)+\tanh\rho\Big(3\hat{I}_{200}(\hat{\Lambda},\xi){-}\hat{I}_{220}(\hat{\Lambda},\xi)\Big)={\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}\tanh\rho.

(2.48)

Obviously, an additional equation of motion for
{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}
is now needed. It is derived from the RTA Boltzmann equation using the standard procedure (see Ref.
[
55
]
):
Writing
f=f_{a}+\delta\tilde{f}
, Eq. (
2.7
) gives the following evolution equation for
\delta\tilde{f}
:

\partial_{\rho}\delta\tilde{f}=\frac{f_{\text{eq}}-f_{a}-\delta\tilde{f}}{\hat{\tau}_{r}}-\partial_{\rho}f_{a}

(2.49)

where from Eqs. (
2.21
) – (
2.22
)

\begin{split}\partial_{\rho}f_{a}=\biggl(\frac{\partial_{\rho}{\hat{\Lambda}}}{\hat{\Lambda}^{2}}E_{\mathrm{RS}}+\frac{1}{\hat{\Lambda}\,E_{\mathrm{RS}}}\Big(\!\tanh\rho\frac{\hat{p}^{2}_{\Omega}}{{\cosh^{2}}\rho}-\frac{\partial_{\rho}{\xi}}{2}\,\hat{p}^{2}_{\eta}\Big)\!\biggr)f_{a}.\end{split}

(2.50)

With this the residual shear stress
{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}\equiv{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}^{\eta\eta}
evolves as

\begin{split}\partial_{\rho}{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}=\partial_{\rho}\int_{\hat{p}}\hat{p}^{\langle\eta}\hat{p}^{\eta\rangle}\delta\tilde{f}=&-\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathrm{RS}}{+}{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}}{\hat{\tau}_{r}}-\tanh\rho\left(\frac{4}{3}{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}{+}\tilde{I}_{240}\right)-\frac{\partial_{\rho}{\hat{\Lambda}}}{\hat{\Lambda}^{2}}\,\left(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{221}{-}\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{201}\right)\\
&-\frac{\tanh\rho}{\hat{\Lambda}}\left(\frac{4}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{42-1}{-}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{44-1}-\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{40-1}\right)\\
&+\frac{\partial_{\rho}{\xi}}{2\hat{\Lambda}}\,\left(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{44-1}{-}\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{42-1}\right).\end{split}

(2.51)

The anisotropic integrals
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{nlr}
are defined in Appendix
A.1
, Eq. (
A.8
), and
\tilde{I}_{240}
is the
\delta\tilde{f}
contribution to
I_{240}
.
Equation (
2.51
) is exact but not closed without an approximation for the residual deviation
\delta\tilde{f}
in the integral
\tilde{I}_{240}
.
We here use the 14-moment approximation for
\delta\tilde{f}
. Its specific form for our present situation is derived in Appendix
A.2
:

\delta\tilde{f}_{14}=\left[\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}\,\hat{u}\cdot\hat{p}+\frac{4}{3}\hat{\omega}(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{p})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}\Big(3(\hat{z}\cdot\hat{p})^{2}{-}(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{p})^{2}\Big)\right]f_{a}\,,

(2.52)

where (with
\alpha_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)
,
\beta_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)
,
\gamma_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)
and
\kappa_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)
given in Eqs. (
A.19
) – (
A.22
))

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\alpha_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{2}(\hat{\Lambda})}\,,\qquad\hat{\beta}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\beta_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{3}(\hat{\Lambda})}\,,\qquad\hat{\omega}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\gamma_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{4}(\hat{\Lambda})}\,,\qquad\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\kappa_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{4}(\hat{\Lambda})}.

(2.53a)

Using this approximation for
\delta\tilde{f}
the calculation of the term
\tilde{I}_{240}
in Eq. (
2.51
) is straightforward. After some algebra, we arrive at the following closed evolution equation for the residual shear stress
{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}
:

\begin{split}\partial_{\rho}{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}=&-\frac{\hat{\pi}_{\mathrm{RS}}{+}{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}}{\hat{\tau}_{r}}-\tanh\rho\left[\frac{4}{3}{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}+\hat{\alpha}\,\hat{I}_{240}-\hat{\beta}\,\hat{I}_{340}+\frac{4}{3}\hat{\omega}\,\hat{I}_{440}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}\left(3\,\hat{I}_{460}{-}\hat{I}_{440}\right)\,\right]\\
&-\frac{\partial_{\rho}{\hat{\Lambda}}}{\hat{\Lambda}^{2}}\,\left(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{221}{-}\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{201}\right)-\frac{\tanh\rho}{\hat{\Lambda}}\left(\frac{4}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{42-1}{-}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{44-1}-\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{40-1}\right)\\
&+\frac{\partial_{\rho}{\xi}}{2\hat{\Lambda}}\,\left(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{44-1}{-}\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{42-1}\right).\end{split}

(2.54)

The evolution equations (
2.48
), (
2.46
) and (
2.54
) define the NLO NRS prescription for anisotropic hydrodynamics subject to Gubser flow. Note that the NLO residual dissipative corrections do not affect the evolution of the momentum anisotropy parameter
\xi
which remains the same as in the leading-order NRS treatment of Ref.
[
122
]
. The NLO corrections only modify the evolution of the energy density
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and pressure anisotropy
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\sim\hat{\pi}=\hat{\pi}_{\mathrm{RS}}+{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}
.

We conclude this subsection by noting that, due to the appearance of non-hydrodynamic higher order moments
\hat{I}_{nlq}
in the NRS and NLO NRS prescriptions, the latter lead to evolution equations that explicitly refer to the evolution of the microscopic momentum anisotropy parameter
\xi
and cannot be formulated purely macroscopically. This sets them apart from the viscous hydrodynamic and
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching anisotropic hydrodynamic formulations. It is therefore not clear how to generalize the NRS and NLO NRS prescriptions to strongly coupled situations where a microscopic kinetic description in terms of quasiparticle distribution functions is not possible.

2.2.6
Exact solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation

For Gubser flow, the RTA Boltzmann equation (
2.7
) is solved exactly by
[
133
,
132
]

f(\rho;\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2},\hat{p}_{\eta})=D(\rho,\rho_{0})f_{0}(\rho_{0};\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2},\hat{p}_{\eta})\,+\,\frac{1}{5\bar{\eta}}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho}d\rho^{\prime}\,D(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\,\hat{T}(\rho^{\prime})\,f_{\text{eq}}(\rho^{\prime};\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2},\hat{p}_{\eta})\,,

(2.55)

where
D(\rho_{2},\rho_{1}){\,=\,}\exp\!\left[-\frac{1}{5\bar{\eta}}\int_{\rho_{1}}^{\rho_{2}}d\rho^{\prime\prime}\,\hat{T}(\rho^{\prime\prime})\right]
is the damping function and
f_{0}
is the initial distribution at de Sitter time
\rho_{0}
, for which we take an RS distribution (
2.21
) with initial effective temperature
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}
and momentum anisotropy
\xi_{0}
:

\!\!\!\!\!f_{0}(\rho_{0};\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2},\hat{p}_{\eta})=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{\hat{\Lambda}_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2}}{\cosh^{2}\!\rho_{0}}+(1{+}\xi_{0})\hat{p}_{\eta}^{2}}\,\right]\,.

(2.56)

From the exact solution (
2.55
) one can compute the exact evolution of the energy density
\hat{\mathcal{E}}=\hat{I}_{200}
and shear stress
\hat{\pi}=\hat{I}_{220}-\frac{1}{3}\hat{I}_{200}

[
133
,
132
]
:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\rho)=\,&D(\rho,\rho_{0})\Big(\frac{\cosh\rho_{0}}{\cosh\rho}\Big)^{\!4}\,\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{RS}}\big(\hat{\Lambda}_{0},\xi_{\mathrm{FS}}(\rho;\rho_{0},\xi_{0})\big)\\
&+\frac{1}{5\bar{\eta}}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho}d\rho^{\prime}\,D(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\,\hat{T}(\rho^{\prime})\Big(\frac{\cosh\rho^{\prime}}{\cosh\rho}\Big)^{\!4}\,\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{RS}}\big(\hat{T}(\rho^{\prime}),\xi_{\mathrm{FS}}(\rho;\rho^{\prime},0)\big),\end{split}

(2.57)

\begin{split}\hat{\pi}(\rho)=\,&D(\rho,\rho_{0})\Big(\frac{\cosh\rho_{0}}{\cosh\rho}\Big)^{\!4}\,\hat{\pi}_{\mathrm{RS}}\big(\hat{\Lambda}_{0},\xi_{\mathrm{FS}}(\rho;\rho_{0},\xi_{0})\big)\\
&+\frac{1}{5\bar{\eta}}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho}d\rho^{\prime}\,D(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\,\hat{T}(\rho^{\prime})\Big(\frac{\cosh\rho^{\prime}}{\cosh\rho}\Big)^{\!4}\,\hat{\pi}_{\mathrm{RS}}\big(\hat{T}(\rho^{\prime}),\xi_{\mathrm{FS}}(\rho;\rho^{\prime},0)\big).\end{split}

(2.58)

Here we defined
\xi_{\mathrm{FS}}(\rho;\rho_{\alpha},\xi_{\alpha})=-1+(1+\xi_{\alpha})\big(\frac{\cosh\rho_{\alpha}}{\cosh\rho}\big)^{2}
. We solve the above integral equations for
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and
\hat{\pi}
numerically using the method described in Refs.
[
158
,
159
,
101
,
132
]
. The temperature is then obtained from the energy density by Landau matching:
\hat{T}=\big(\frac{1}{3}\pi^{2}\hat{\mathcal{E}}\big)^{1/4}
. When discussing the results in the following section we plot the evolution of the temperature rather than the energy density.

2.3
Results

In this section we compare the numerical results obtained from the five different approaches discussed in the preceding section (second-order viscous hydrodynamics (DNMR), anisotropic hydrodynamics based on the
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching, NRS and NLO NRS prescriptions, and the exact solution of the Boltzmann equation) for the evolution of the temperature and normalized shear stress.

For the benefit of the reader we summarize the set of equations that are being solved in each of the five cases:

1.

Viscous hydrodynamics (DNMR):
qquations (
2.19
) for
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
, together with
\hat{\mathcal{E}}=3\,\hat{T}^{4}/\pi^{2}
.

2.

Anisotropic hydrodynamics (
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching):
equations (
2.19a
) and (
2.39
) together with
\hat{\mathcal{E}}{\,=\,}3\,\hat{T}^{4}/\pi^{2}
and Eq. (
2.41
) to obtain
\hat{T}
and
\xi
from
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
.

3.

Anisotropic hydrodynamics (NRS):
equations (
2.42
) and (
2.46
) for
\hat{\Lambda}
and
\xi
, from which we get
\hat{T}
and
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
using Eqs. (
2.34
) and (
2.41
).

4.

Anisotropic hydrodynamics (NLO NRS):
equations (
2.46
) and (
2.48
) for
\hat{\Lambda}
and
\xi
, together with Eq. (
2.54
) for the residual shear stress
{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}}
and Eqs. (
2.34
) and (
2.41
) to get
\hat{T}
and the leading order
\hat{\bar{\pi}}(\xi)
from
\hat{\Lambda}
and
\xi
.

5.

Exact solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation:
equations (
2.57
) (rewritten in terms of
\hat{T}
) and (
2.58
).

All five models are started at an initial de Sitter time
\rho_{0}{\,=\,}-10
with initial temperature
\hat{T}_{0}=0.002
and run for three choices of the specific shear viscosity,
4\pi\hat{\eta}/\hat{\mathcal{S}}=1,\,3,
and 10 (top, middle and bottom row of panels in Figs.
2.1
–
2.3
), and three choices of the initial momentum anisotropy,
\xi_{0}=0,\,100,
and -0.9 (shown in Figs.
2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3
, respectively). We now proceed to discuss these results in detail.

Figure 2.1:
The de Sitter time evolution of the temperature
\hat{T}
and the normalized shear stress
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
for the exact solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation (black solid lines) and four different hydrodynamic approximations: second-order viscous hydrodynamics (DNMR theory, short-dashed magenta lines), anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching (dotted red lines), leading order anisotropic hydrodynamics in the NRS scheme (dash-dotted green lines), and next-to-leading order anisotropic hydrodynamics in the NRS scheme amended by residual viscous corrections (long-dashed blue lines). For the initial momentum distribution we here assumed isotropy, i.e.
\xi_{0}=0
. The top, middle and bottom rows of panels correspond to specific shear viscosity
4\pi\hat{\eta}/\hat{\mathcal{S}}=1
, 3, and 10, respectively. The four columns of plots show, from left to right, the
\rho
evolution of the temperature
\hat{T}
, of the ratio between its hydrodynamic and exact kinetic evolution
\hat{T}/\hat{T}_{\mathrm{exact}}
, of the normalized shear stress
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
, and of the difference between its hydrodynamic and exact kinetic evolution
\hat{\bar{\pi}}-\hat{\bar{\pi}}_{\mathrm{exact}}
.

2.3.1
Evolution of temperature and shear stress

Figure 2.2:
Same as Fig.
2.1
, but for an initially highly oblate momentum distribution,
\xi_{0}=100
.

Figure 2.3:
Same as Fig.
2.1
, but for an initially highly prolate momentum distribution,
\xi_{0}=-0.9
.

Figures
2.1
–
2.3
show the de Sitter time evolution of the temperature
\hat{T}
and of the normalized shear stress
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
, in the first and third column in absolute values, and in the second and fourth column relative to the exact solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation. It is obvious that all three anisotropic hydrodynamic schemes studied here vastly outperform standard second-order viscous hydrodynamics. Over the range of de Sitter times studied here, anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching never deviates from the exact solution by more than a few percent. The results from the leading-order NRS scheme (for which the momentum anisotropy parameter is not matched to the macroscopic pressure anisotropy) performs slightly worse, but not dramatically so. Once the residual shear stress caused by the non-optimal
\xi
-evolution in this approach is added to the formalism at next-to-leading order, the evolution of both
\hat{T}
and
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
agrees almost perfectly with that in the
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching scheme.

For all of the anisotropic hydrodynamic schemes the normalized shear stress
\hat{\bar{\pi}}
correctly approaches the asymptotic free-streaming value
\frac{1}{2}
predicted by the RTA Boltzmann equation
[
132
]
, in contrast to standard second-order viscous fluid dynamics; the asymptotic temperature lies a few percent above the exact value. The asymptotic behavior at large de Sitter times is almost independent of the initial momentum anisotropy
\xi_{0}
: due to the rapid expansion the system quickly loses its memory of the initial state. As the specific shear viscosity increases (corresponding to increasing values of the microscopic relaxation time), the deviations between the hydrodynamic evolution of the macroscopic observables and that extracted from the exact solution of the Boltzmann equation grow a bit. However, even for
\hat{\eta}/\hat{\mathcal{S}}
values 10 times larger than the “minimal” value
1/(4\pi)

[
160
,
161
]
the deviations from the exact solution stay below 4% for both the temperature ratio
\hat{T}/\hat{T}_{\text{exact}}
and the difference
\hat{\bar{\pi}}-\hat{\bar{\pi}}_{\text{exact}}
as long as the
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching scheme is employed.

2.4
Summary

In this chapter we studied conformal systems undergoing Gubser flow that admit simultaneously a kinetic description via the RTA Boltzmann equation and a macroscopic hydrodynamic description. We reviewed the standard second-order viscous hydrodynamic formulation and compared it with three variants of anisotropic hydrodynamics, each of them implementing a different closing scheme. In the comparison with the evolution of the energy-momentum tensor obtained from the exact kinetic solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation, we found anisotropic hydrodynamics with the
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}
–matching scheme to be the most accurate macroscopic realization of the underlying kinetic theory, confirming observations made previously in Ref.
[
118
]
for Bjorken flow. In this approach the evolution of the microscopic momentum anisotropy parameter
\xi
is matched to the expansion-driven macroscopic pressure anisotropy
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{L}-\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}
. We also showed that an alternative prescription proposed in Ref.
[
122
]
where the
\xi
evolution does not match the pressure anisotropy can be made equally accurate, albeit with more work by solving a larger set of equations, by accounting for the residual shear stress associated with the non-optimal
\xi
evolution (NLO NRS scheme). Both procedures lead to descriptions that deviate from the exact results by at most a few percent.

We emphasize that, in contrast to the NLO NRS scheme, anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching can be formulated entirely macroscopically, without explicitly referring to the underlying microscopic kinetic description and its parameters. Here we showed that the momentum anisotropy parameter
\xi
can be equivalently replaced by the pressure anisotropy
\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
. This puts anisotropic hydrodynamics on the same footing as second-order viscous hydrodynamics in that it can be generalized from conformal systems with Gubser symmetry to non-conformal systems undergoing arbitrary expansion. To get there, we will now apply the techniques from the
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching scheme to derive new hydrodynamic equations for the anisotropically expanding quark-gluon plasma.

Chapter 3
Anisotropic fluid dynamics for the quark-gluon plasma

Here we generalize anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching to model the far-from-equilibrium dynamics of the non-conformal quark-gluon plasma in (3+1)–dimensions for various initial conditions. In addition to the large pressure anisotropies produced in the early stages of heavy-ion collisions, there may also be large bulk viscous effects present when the quark-gluon plasma converts to hadrons. Near the pseudocritical temperature
T_{c}
, the bulk viscosity
\zeta/\mathcal{S}
is expected to peak strongly due to large critical fluctuations.
1
1

1

The overall magnitude of the bulk viscous pressure ultimately depends on critical slowing-down, where the response to global expansion is delayed by the same critical fluctuations
[
162
,
163
,
164
]
. The strength of this hydrodynamic response for the non-equilibrium quark-gluon plasma, however, is not well understood.

In our model, we capture these large dissipative flows non-perturbatively by extending the Landau matching to both the longitudinal and transverse pressures
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
(or equivalently the diagonal shear component
\pi^{zz}\propto\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
and bulk viscous pressure
\Pi
). We then treat the residual shear stresses perturbatively, similar to what is done for
\pi^{\mu\nu}
and
\Pi
in second-order viscous hydrodynamics. To construct the framework, we derive the anisotropic hydrodynamic equations from the relativistic Boltzmann equation for a system of quasiparticles with a medium-dependent mass
m(T)
. With the generalized Landau matching conditions, we can parametrize the anisotropic transport coefficients of this kinetic theory model in terms of the QCD energy density
\mathcal{E}
and deviations of
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
from the QCD equilibrium pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})
(the shear and bulk viscosities are modeled phenomenologically). This allows us to evolve the relaxation equations macroscopically in the same manner as standard viscous hydrodynamics.

This chapter is based on material previously published in Ref.
[
120
]
.

3.1
Anisotropic hydrodynamics

3.1.1
Energy-momentum tensor decomposition

Similar to the Gubser flow, the shear stress
\pi^{\mu\nu}
in ultrarelativistic heavy-ion collisions is initially highly anisotropic due to strongly different longitudinal and transverse expansion rates. This leads to large differences
\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
between the longitudinal and transverse pressures, especially at the onset of the collision. Therefore, the hydrodynamic equations for the non-equilibrium quark-gluon plasma are best formulated in the basis
\{u^{\mu}
,
z^{\mu}
,
\Xi^{\mu\nu}\}
used in the previous chapter. In ordinary Minkowski spacetime
x^{\mu}=(t,x,y,z)
, the energy-momentum tensor is decomposed as (suppressing the spacetime dependence)

T^{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{E}u^{\mu}u^{\nu}+\mathcal{P}_{L}z^{\mu}z^{\nu}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}\Xi^{\mu\nu}+2W^{(\mu}_{\perp z}z^{\nu)}+\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}.

(3.1)

The round parentheses around pairs of Lorentz indices indicate symmetrization:
W^{(\mu}_{\perp z}z^{\nu)}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\bigl(W^{\mu}_{\perp z}z^{\nu}+W^{\nu}_{\perp z}z^{\mu}\bigr)
. The anisotropic decomposition (
3.1
) clearly separates the pressures
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
from the other dissipative components. Given an arbitrary energy-momentum tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
, the anisotropic hydrodynamic quantities appearing in this decomposition can be obtained by the following projections:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=u_{\mu}u_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.2a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}

\displaystyle=z_{\mu}z_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.2b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\Xi_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.2c)

\displaystyle W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\,

\displaystyle=-\Xi^{\mu}_{\alpha}T^{\alpha\nu}z_{\nu}\,,

(3.2d)

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\Xi_{\alpha\beta}^{\mu\nu}T^{\alpha\beta}\,.

(3.2e)

In the last line we introduced the symmetric traceless transverse projection tensor
\Xi^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\Xi^{\mu}_{\alpha}\Xi^{\nu}_{\beta}+\Xi^{\nu}_{\beta}\Xi^{\mu}_{\alpha}-\Xi^{\mu\nu}\Xi_{\alpha\beta}\big)
. The corresponding transverse shear stress tensor
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
describes two shear stress degrees of freedom that account for momentum diffusion currents along the transverse directions. It is traceless and orthogonal to both the fluid velocity
u^{\mu}
and the direction of the pressure anisotropy
z^{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu}_{\perp,\mu}=u_{\mu}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}=z_{\mu}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}=0.

(3.3)

Another two shear stress degrees of freedom are encoded in the longitudinal-momentum diffusion current
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
which is orthogonal to both
u^{\mu}
and
z^{\mu}
:

\displaystyle u_{\mu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}=z_{\mu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}=0.

(3.4)

The remaining fifth (and largest) shear stress component is given by
\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
. Altogether, the five independent components of the standard shear stress tensor
\pi^{\mu\nu}
are related to those in the anisotropic decomposition (
3.1
) by

\pi^{\mu\nu}=\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}+2\,W^{(\mu}_{\perp z}z^{\nu)}+\frac{1}{3}\big(\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}\big)\big(2z^{\mu}z^{\nu}{-}\Xi^{\mu\nu}\big)

(3.5)

while the bulk viscous pressure
\Pi
is related to the longitudinal and transverse pressures by

\Pi=\frac{2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L}}{3}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}.

(3.6)

Here the equilibrium pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}
is not an independent degree of freedom but related to the energy density
\mathcal{E}
through the equation of state of the fluid,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})
.

3.1.2
Hydrodynamic evolution equations

Four of the ten evolution equations that control the dynamics of the energy-momentum tensor are obtained from
the conservation laws for energy and momentum

\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0.

(3.7)

Projecting with
u_{\nu}
on the temporal direction in the LRF provides an evolution equation for the LRF energy density:

\dot{\mathcal{E}}+(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\perp})\theta_{\perp}+(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L})\theta_{L}+W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\bigl(D_{z}u_{\mu}{-}z_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}u^{\nu}\bigr)-\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp,{\mu\nu}}=0.

(3.8)

Here and below a dot over or a
D
in front of a quantity denotes the co-moving time derivative, e.g.
D\mathcal{E}\equiv\dot{\mathcal{E}}\equiv u^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{E}
.
\theta_{L}=z_{\mu}D_{z}u^{\mu}
is the scalar longitudinal expansion rate and
\theta_{\perp}=\nabla_{\perp,\mu}u^{\mu}
is the scalar transverse expansion rate. The LRF longitudinal derivative and transverse gradient are written as
D_{z}=-z^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
and
\nabla_{\perp,\mu}=\Xi_{\mu}^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}
, respectively.
\sigma_{\perp,{\mu\nu}}=\Xi^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\beta}
is the transverse velocity-shear tensor.

The longitudinal projection
z_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
yields an equation for the longitudinal acceleration of the fluid in the LRF:

(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L})z_{\mu}\dot{u}^{\mu}=-D_{z}\mathcal{P}_{L}+(\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp})\tilde{\theta}_{\perp}-W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\dot{u}_{\mu}-2W^{\mu}_{\perp z}D_{z}z_{\mu}+\nabla_{\perp,\mu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}+\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\tilde{\sigma}_{\perp,\mu\nu}.

(3.9)

Here
\tilde{\theta}_{\perp}\equiv\nabla_{\perp,\mu}z^{\mu}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{\perp,{\mu\nu}}=\Xi^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}z_{\beta}
.
2
2

2

Generically we use tildes to indicate quantities involving derivatives of
z^{\mu}
instead of
u^{\mu}
.

An equation for the transverse acceleration is obtained from the transverse projection
\Xi^{\alpha}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
:

\begin{split}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\perp})\Xi^{\alpha}_{\nu}\dot{u}^{\nu}=\,&\nabla_{\perp}^{\alpha}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}+(\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp})\Xi^{\alpha}_{\nu}D_{z}z^{\nu}-W^{\alpha}_{\perp z}\Big(\frac{3}{2}\tilde{\theta}_{\perp}{-}z_{\nu}\dot{u}^{\nu}\Big)\\
&-W_{\perp z,\nu}(\tilde{\sigma}^{\alpha\nu}_{\perp}{-}\tilde{\omega}^{\alpha\nu}_{\perp})+\Xi^{\alpha}_{\nu}D_{z}W^{\nu}_{\perp z}+\pi^{\alpha\nu}_{\perp}(\dot{u}_{\nu}{+}D_{z}z_{\nu})-\Xi^{\alpha}_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}.\end{split}

(3.10)

Here
\tilde{\omega}_{\perp}^{\alpha\nu}\equiv\Xi^{\alpha\mu}\Xi^{\nu\beta}\partial_{[\beta}z_{\mu]}
where the square brackets indicate antisymmetrization:
\partial_{[\beta}z_{\mu]}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\partial_{\beta}z_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}z_{\beta}\bigr)
. Equations (
3.8
) – (
3.10
) agree (after adjustment of notation) with Eqs. (146) – (148) in Ref.
[
117
]
.

3.2
Dissipative relaxation equations

To close the system of equations, we need six additional relaxation equations for
\mathcal{P}_{L}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
,
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
. Their dynamics is not controlled by macroscopic conservation laws but by microscopic interactions among the fluid’s constituents. We will here derive them by assuming a weakly-coupled dilute fluid whose microscopic physics can be described by the relativistic Boltzmann-Vlasov equation for a single particle species with a medium-dependent mass:

p^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f+m\,\partial^{\mu}m\,\partial_{\mu}^{(p)}f=C[f].

(3.11)

Here
f(x,p)
is the single particle distribution function,
C[f]
is the collision kernel,
m(x)
is the medium-dependent effective mass, and
\partial_{\mu}^{(p)}
is the momentum derivative.

3.2.1
Anisotropic distribution function

Similar to the decomposition (
2.20
) introduced in Chapter
2
, we expand the distribution function around a leading-order anisotropic distribution:

f(x,p)=f_{a}(x,p)+\delta\tilde{f}(x,p)\,.

(3.12)

In this case, however, the Romatschke–Strickland distribution is not conformal
[
121
,
145
,
116
]
:

f_{a}(x,p)=\left(\exp\left[\frac{\sqrt{\Omega_{\mu\nu}(x)\,p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}}{\Lambda(x)}\,\right]+\Theta\right)^{-1}\,,

(3.13)

where
\Lambda(x)
is the effective temperature and
\Theta\in(0,\pm 1)
accounts for Boltzmann, Fermi–Dirac, and Bose–Einstein statistics, respectively. The leading-order momentum anisotropy is encoded in the ellipsoidal tensor

\Omega_{\mu\nu}(x)=u_{\mu}(x)u_{\nu}(x)-\xi_{\perp}(x)\Xi_{\mu\nu}(x)+\xi_{L}(x)z_{\mu}(x)z_{\nu}(x).

(3.14)

For non-conformal plasmas
3
3

3

In the conformal limit
m\to 0
, the momentum anisotropy parameters reduce to
(\xi_{\perp},\xi_{L})\to(0,\xi)
.
, there are two spacetime dependent anisotropy parameters
\xi_{L}
and
\xi_{\perp}
that deform the longitudinal and transverse momentum space, respectively. The tensor contraction
p\cdot\Omega\cdot p
can be rewritten as

p\cdot\Omega\cdot p=m^{2}+(1{+}\xi_{\perp})p^{2}_{\perp,\mathrm{LRF}}+(1{+}\xi_{L})p^{2}_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}\,,

(3.15)

where
p_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}=-z\cdot p
is the LRF longitudinal momentum and
p^{2}_{\perp,\mathrm{LRF}}=-p\cdot\Xi\cdot p
is the square of the LRF transverse momentum. The difference
\xi_{L}-\xi_{\perp}
can be attributed to a manifestation of shear stress (resulting in a difference between the longitudinal and transverse pressures) while the sum
\xi_{L}+\xi_{\perp}
encodes a bulk viscous pressure
[
148
,
115
]
. Introducing the notation
\alpha_{L,\perp}(x)=\bigl(1+\xi_{L,\perp}(x)\bigr)^{-1/2}
, Eq. (
3.13
) can be written in LRF momentum components more conveniently as

f_{a}=\left(\exp\left[\frac{1}{\Lambda}\sqrt{m^{2}+\frac{p^{2}_{\perp,\mathrm{LRF}}}{\alpha_{\perp}^{2}}+\frac{p^{2}_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}}{\alpha_{L}^{2}}}\,\right]+\Theta\right)^{-1}.

(3.16)

To make the decomposition (
3.12
) unique one must specify the three parameters
\bm{\alpha}(x)\equiv\bigl(\alpha_{\perp}(x),\alpha_{L}(x)\bigr)
and
\Lambda(x)
. We proceed as follows: the physical temperature
T
of the system is defined by the LRF energy density via the thermodynamic relation
\mathcal{E}(x)\equiv\mathcal{E}\bigl(T(x)\bigr)
. To relate the effective temperature
\Lambda
to
T
we impose the generalized Landau matching condition

\delta\tilde{\mathcal{E}}\equiv\int_{p}(u\cdot p)^{2}\delta\tilde{f}=0\,.

(3.17)

We define the Lorentz-invariant momentum space integral

\int_{p}=\frac{g}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int d^{4}p\,2\Theta(p^{0})\delta(p^{2}{-}m^{2})=\frac{g}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{E_{p}}\,,

(3.18)

where
g
is a degeneracy factor counting the number of quantum states allowed for a particle with on-shell momentum
p^{\mu}
, and
\Theta(p^{0})
denotes the Heaviside step function. Equation (
3.17
) states that
\Lambda(\bm{\alpha})
must be chosen such that the residual deviation
\delta\tilde{f}
does not contribute to the energy density. This fixes
\Lambda(\bm{\alpha})
as a function of
T
; the two agree in the limit
\bm{\alpha}\to(1,1)
when the anisotropic distribution
f_{a}
reduces to the local-equilibrium distribution
f_{\mathrm{eq}}=\left(\exp\left[u\cdot p/T\right]+\Theta\right)^{-1}
.

The momentum deformation parameters
\alpha_{L,\perp}(x)
are fixed by similar generalized Landau matching conditions for the longitudinal and transverse pressures

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}+\Pi+\pi^{zz}_{\mathrm{LRF}},

(3.19a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}+\Pi-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{zz}_{\mathrm{LRF}}.

(3.19b)

Here
\pi^{zz}_{\mathrm{LRF}}
is the LRF value of the longitudinal diagonal element of the shear stress tensor
\pi^{\mu\nu}
in the decomposition (
3.5
). Note that both the bulk viscous pressure
\Pi
and shear stress component
\pi^{zz}_{\mathrm{LRF}}
are here assumed to be “large” such that they must be accounted for already at leading order, by adjusting the parameters
\alpha_{L,\perp}(x)
accordingly. This is done by demanding

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{L}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{p}(-z\cdot p)^{2}\delta\tilde{f}=0,

(3.20a)

\displaystyle\delta\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int_{p}(-p\cdot\Xi\cdot p)\delta\tilde{f}=0.

(3.20b)

By imposing these conditions,
\alpha_{L,\perp}(x)
are adjusted such that the longitudinal and transverse pressures
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
are everywhere fully accounted for by the leading-order distribution
f_{a}
, with zero residual contributions from
\delta\tilde{f}
. This is an application of the anisotropic matching scheme proposed by Tinti in
[
116
]
and generalizes the
P_{L}
–matching scheme used in Chapter
2

[
118
,
102
]
to both
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
(or, equivalently, to the pressure anisotropy
\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}\sim\pi^{zz}_{\mathrm{LRF}}
and bulk viscous pressure
\Pi
). In this matching scheme, the
\delta\tilde{f}
correction generates only the residual dissipative flows described by
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
, which break the cylindrical symmetry of the distribution function in the LRF and account for the remaining four smaller components of the shear stress tensor
\pi^{\mu\nu}
in Eq. (
3.5
).

With the matching conditions (
3.17
) and (
3.20
a,b) we have the following kinetic theory expressions for the energy density and the longitudinal and transverse pressures:
4
4

4

The superscript
(k)
on macroscopic quantities indicates their kinetic theory definition for a gas
of weakly-interacting quasiparticles. The purpose of this notation will become clear later when we introduce
a more realistic equation of state.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\int_{p}(u\cdot p)^{2}f_{a}=\mathcal{I}_{2000},

(3.21a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\int_{p}(-z\cdot p)^{2}f_{a}=\mathcal{I}_{2200},

(3.21b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{p}(-p\cdot\Xi\cdot p)f_{a}=\mathcal{I}_{2010}.

(3.21c)

The anisotropic integrals
\mathcal{I}_{nrqs}
over the leading-order distribution function
f_{a}
that appear in these equations are defined in Eq. (
B.1
).

3.2.2
Relaxation equations I

The relaxation equations for the dissipative flows are obtained by expressing the latter as moments of the distribution function and using the Boltzmann equation to describe its evolution, using the decomposition (
3.12
) and treating
\delta\tilde{f}
as a small perturbation. We start from

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}_{L}

\displaystyle=D\int_{p}(-z\cdot p)^{2}f_{a}\,,

(3.22a)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D\int_{p}(-p\cdot\Xi\cdot p)f_{a}\,,

(3.22b)

\displaystyle\dot{W}^{\{\mu\}}_{\perp z}

\displaystyle=\Xi^{\mu}_{\nu}D\int_{p}(-z\cdot p)\,p^{\{\nu\}}\delta\tilde{f}\,,

(3.22c)

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\Xi^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}D\int_{p}p^{\{\alpha}p^{\beta\}}\delta\tilde{f}\,,

(3.22d)

where we defined the compact notations
[
117
]

\displaystyle a^{\{\mu\}}\equiv\Xi^{\mu\nu}a_{\nu},\quad b^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}\equiv\Xi^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}b^{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle\dot{a}^{\{\mu\}}\equiv\Xi^{\mu\nu}\dot{a}_{\nu},\quad\dot{b}^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}\equiv\Xi^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\dot{b}^{\alpha\beta}

(3.23)

for the spatially transverse (in the LRF) components of a vector
a^{\mu}
or its LRF time derivative
\dot{a}^{\mu}
and the spatially transverse and traceless part of a tensor
b^{\mu\nu}
or its LRF time derivative
\dot{b}^{\mu\nu}
. After moving the time derivative
D
on the r.h.s. under the integral until it hits the distribution function
f_{a}
or
\delta\tilde{f}
, we use the decomposition
f=f_{a}+\delta\tilde{f}
together with

\partial_{\mu}=u_{\mu}D+z_{\mu}D_{z}+\nabla_{\perp,\mu}

(3.24)

to rewrite the Boltzmann-Vlasov equation (
3.11
) in the form

\begin{split}\dot{f}_{a}+\delta\dot{\tilde{f}}=\,&\frac{C[f]-m\,\partial^{\mu}m\,\partial_{\mu}^{(p)}f}{u\cdot p}+\frac{(-z\cdot p)D_{z}f_{a}-p^{\{\mu\}}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}f_{a}}{u\cdot p}\\
&+\frac{(-z\cdot p)D_{z}\delta\tilde{f}-p^{\{\mu\}}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}\delta\tilde{f}}{u\cdot p}\,.\end{split}

(3.25)

Closing this equation requires an approximation for
\delta\tilde{f}
. We here use the 14–moment approximation.

3.2.3
The 14–moment approximation

The 14–moment approximation derives its name from approximating
\delta\tilde{f}
in terms of its 14 momentum moments with
p^{\mu}
and
p^{\mu}p^{\nu}

[
165
,
166
]
. In our case the choice of the Landau frame, together with the generalized matching conditions (
3.17
) and (
3.20
a,b) and the absence of currents related to conserved charges, eliminate ten of these moments, leaving only four independent moments to construct
\delta\tilde{f}
. These need to be matched to the residual dissipative flows
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
, which each have two degrees of freedom. The 14–moment approximation for
\delta\tilde{f}
can thus be written as
[
117
]

\frac{\delta\tilde{f}}{f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}}=c_{\perp}^{\{\mu\}}(-z\cdot p)p_{\{\mu\}}+c_{\perp}^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}p_{\{\mu}\,p_{\nu\}}\,,

(3.26)

where
\bar{f}_{a}=1-\Theta f_{a}
. The coefficients
c_{\perp}^{\{\mu\}}
and
c_{\perp}^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}
are computed by substituting Eq. (
3.26
) into the kinetic theory definitions of
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
,

\displaystyle W^{\mu}_{\perp z}

\displaystyle=\int_{p}(-z\cdot p)p^{\{\mu\}}\delta\tilde{f}\,,

(3.27a)

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\int_{p}p^{\{\mu}p^{\nu\}}\delta\tilde{f}\,,

(3.27b)

and decoupling the resulting set of linear equations:

\displaystyle c_{\perp}^{\{\mu\}}=-\frac{W^{\mu}_{\perp z}}{\mathcal{J}_{4210}}\,,\qquad c_{\perp}^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}=\frac{\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}}{2\mathcal{J}_{4020}}\,.

(3.28)

The anisotropic integrals
\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}
appearing in these expressions
are defined in Eq. (
B.2
). As expected, the coefficients are directly proportional to the residual dissipative flows:

\delta\tilde{f}=\left(-(-z\cdot p)\frac{p_{\{\mu\}}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}}{\mathcal{J}_{4210}}+\frac{p_{\{\mu}\,p_{\nu\}}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}}{2\,\mathcal{J}_{4020}}\right)f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}\,.

(3.29)

3.2.4
Relaxation equations II

Substituting the 14–moment approximation (
3.29
) for
\delta\tilde{f}
into Eqs. (
3.22
) and (
3.25
), simplifying some of the resulting terms by integrating by parts, and enforcing the generalized matching conditions, some algebra yields the following dissipative relaxation equations:
5
5

5

In deriving these equations one encounters terms involving the co-moving time derivative

\dot{m}
of the temperature-dependent quasiparticle mass that arise from the second term
on the l.h.s. of the Boltzmann equation (
3.11
). We eliminate them by using the
chain rule
\dot{m}=(dm/d\mathcal{E})\,\dot{\mathcal{E}}
where we take
dm/d\mathcal{E}
as external input
from the quasiparticle model discussed below and use Eq. (
3.8
) for
\dot{\mathcal{E}}
.
Equations (
3.30
a-d) are found after combining the terms on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
3.8
) with
other terms involving the same dissipative forces. In doing so we also neglect contributions of

\mathcal{O}(\text{Kn}\,\tilde{\text{R}}^{-1}_{i}\tilde{\text{R}}^{-1}_{j})
, where
\tilde{\text{R}}^{-1}_{i}

is the residual inverse Reynolds number associated with the residual components

W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
.

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}_{L}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}{-}\bar{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}}{3\tau_{\pi}/2}+\bar{\zeta}^{L(k)}_{z}\theta_{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{L(k)}_{\perp}\theta_{\perp}-2W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\dot{z}_{\mu}+\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{Wu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}D_{z}u_{\mu}

(3.30a)

\displaystyle+\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{W\perp}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}z_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}u^{\nu}-\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp,{\mu\nu}}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}_{\perp}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}{-}\bar{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}}{\tau_{\Pi}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}}{3\tau_{\pi}}+\bar{\zeta}^{\perp(k)}_{z}\theta_{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{\perp(k)}_{\perp}\theta_{\perp}+W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\dot{z}_{\mu}+\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{Wu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}D_{z}u_{\mu}

(3.30b)

\displaystyle-\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{W\perp}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}z_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}u^{\nu}+\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp,{\mu\nu}}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{W}^{\{\mu\}}_{\perp z}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{W^{\mu}_{\perp z}}{\tau_{\pi}}+2\bar{\eta}^{W}_{u}\Xi^{\mu\nu}D_{z}u_{\nu}-2\bar{\eta}^{W}_{\perp}z_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp}^{\mu}u^{\nu}-\big(\bar{\tau}^{W}_{z}\Xi^{\mu\nu}{+}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\big)\dot{z}_{\nu}-\bar{\lambda}^{W}_{Wu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\theta_{L}

(3.30c)

\displaystyle+\bar{\delta}^{W}_{W}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\theta_{\perp}+\bar{\lambda}^{W}_{W\perp}\sigma_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}W_{\perp z,\nu}+\omega_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}W_{\perp z,\nu}+\bar{\lambda}^{W}_{\pi u}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}D_{z}u_{\nu}

\displaystyle-\bar{\lambda}^{W}_{\pi\perp}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}z_{\lambda}\nabla_{\perp,\nu}u^{\lambda}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}^{\{{\mu\nu}\}}_{\perp}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}}{\tau_{\pi}}+2\bar{\eta}_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}-2W_{\perp z}^{\{\mu}\dot{z}^{\nu\}}+\bar{\lambda}^{\pi}_{\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\theta_{L}-\bar{\delta}^{\pi}_{\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\theta_{\perp}-\bar{\tau}^{\pi}_{\pi}\pi_{\perp}^{\lambda\{\mu}\sigma^{\nu\}}_{\perp,\lambda}

(3.30d)

\displaystyle+2\pi_{\perp}^{\lambda\{\mu}\omega^{\nu\}}_{\perp,\lambda}-\bar{\lambda}^{\pi}_{Wu}W_{\perp z}^{\{\mu}D_{z}u^{\nu\}}+\bar{\lambda}^{\pi}_{W\perp}W_{\perp z}^{\{\mu}z_{\lambda}\nabla_{\perp}^{\nu\}}u^{\lambda}\,.

Here
\bar{\mathcal{P}}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{3}(\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}{+}2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)})
is the average pressure as given by kinetic theory, and
\omega_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\Xi^{\mu}_{\alpha}\Xi^{\nu}_{\beta}\,\partial^{[\beta}u^{\alpha]}
is the transverse vorticity tensor.

The structure of Eqs. (
3.30
a-d) is simpler than that of the corresponding equations derived in Ref.
[
117
]
, not only by the absence of terms coupling to the conserved charge and diffusion currents (which only reflects the simplifying assumptions made here), but also as a result of imposing the generalized Landau matching conditions (
3.17
) and (
3.20
a,b), which optimize the evolution of the anisotropy parameters in
f_{a}
and thus remove additional terms needed in Ref.
[
117
]
to correct their evolution if not chosen optimally in the first place.

The transport coefficients appearing on the right hand sides of Eqs. (
3.30
a-d) are labeled following as much as possible the convention established in Ref.
[
117
]
. Except for the relaxation times they are given in Sec.
4.1.5
.
6
6

6

Please note the superscripts
(k)
on the transport coefficients appearing on the right hand sides of
Eqs. (
3.30
a-b) (also listed in Appendix
B.3
). They reflect the fact that these control the evolution of the kinetic
part of the longitudinal and transverse pressures. For the quasiparticle model introduced in the next section
an additional mean-field enters which modifies these pressures and transport coefficients. The modified
expressions will be denoted without the superscript in Eqs. (
3.48
) – (
3.49
) and in Chapter
4
.

Generically they involve the “anisotropic thermodynamic integrals” over the anisotropic distribution function
f_{a}
given in Appendix
B.1
. Their validity, as well as specific relations between some of the transport coefficients listed in Chapter
4
, depends on the applicability of relativistic kinetic theory of a gas of weakly-interacting quasiparticles as the underlying microscopic theory, which is not guaranteed for quark-gluon plasma. Their generalization to a more realistic microscopic theory of QCD medium dynamics requires much additional work. For now, we will use the expressions given in Chapter
4
as order-of-magnitude estimates and placeholders for future sets of transport coefficients.

Equations (
3.30
a-d) also involve two microscopic time scales: the bulk relaxation time
\tau_{\Pi}
controls the relaxation of the bulk viscous pressure
\Pi
whereas the shear relaxation time
\tau_{\pi}
drives the relaxation of both the large shear stress component
\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
and the smaller ones described by
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
. That all shear stress components have the same relaxation time even if some of them become large is a model assumption that may be corrected in future improved calculations of the transport coefficients for strongly anisotropically expanding quark-gluon plasma.

Formally, the relaxation times arise from a linearization of the collision term around the local equilibrium distribution
f_{\mathrm{eq}}
(with temperature computed from the energy density):

C[f]=\frac{p\cdot u(x)}{\tau_{r}(x)}\left(f_{\mathrm{eq}}(x,p)-f(x,p)\right).

(3.31)

Literal use of the relaxation time approximation (RTA)
[
64
]
gives
\tau_{\pi}=\tau_{\Pi}=\tau_{r}
. However, strong coupling in the quark-gluon plasma in the temperature regime just above the quark-hadron phase transition, as well as critical behavior near that phase transition, lead to very different temperature dependences of the bulk and shear viscosities and their associated relaxation times in QCD, especially around
T_{c}

[
68
,
167
,
168
,
69
,
169
]
. In particular, the bulk relaxation time
\tau_{\Pi}
is expected to be affected by “critical slowing down”
[
168
,
162
,
163
]
, i.e. it should exhibit a strong peak near
T_{c}
. Since large bulk viscous effects near
T_{c}
are one of the main motivations for this thesis, we feel compelled to account for them by introducing two different relaxation times
\tau_{\pi}
and
\tau_{\Pi}
, and tying them to phenomenologically parametrized shear and bulk viscosities
\eta
and
\zeta
by taking the standard kinetic theory relations
[
55
]

\tau_{\pi}=\eta/\beta_{\pi}\,,\qquad\tau_{\Pi}=\zeta/\beta_{\Pi}.

(3.32)

The (temperature dependent) isotropic thermodynamic integrals
\beta_{\pi}
and
\beta_{\Pi}
appearing in these relations are given further below in Eq. (
3.58
). The shear and bulk viscosities are first-order transport coefficients that appear in standard viscous hydroynamics – they are also present here through the relaxation times
\tau_{\pi}
and
\tau_{\Pi}
. When comparing anisotropic with viscous hydrodynamics further below in Sec.
3.4
we will do so by using the same viscosities in both approaches (the coefficients
\beta_{\pi}
and
\beta_{\Pi}
, however, can vary).

3.3
Anisotropic equation of state

While the relaxation equations (
3.30
a-d) were derived from the Boltzmann equation, the equations remain structurally unchanged for strongly coupled fluids. They are purely macroscopic, i.e. all terms on the r.h.s. have the form of some macroscopic driving force (proportional to the Knudsen or inverse Reynolds numbers or products thereof) multiplied by some transport coefficient. The kinetic origin of these equations is hidden in these transport coefficients. Applying the equations to strongly coupled fluids requires only that these transport coefficients, along with the equation of state relating the energy density and equilibrium pressure, are swapped out accordingly.

For the time being most of the transport coefficients of hot and dense QCD matter are still essentially unknown. While the shear and bulk viscosities will be taken as parameters whose functional forms are modeled phenomenologically and fitted to experimental observables, the remaining transport coefficients will be approximated using kinetic theory, for reasons of consistency with our derivation of the relaxation equations. Their evaluation requires microscopic kinetic inputs, namely the parameters
(\Lambda,\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L})
and quasiparticle mass
m
characterizing the anisotropic distribution function
f_{a}
. However, for the quark-gluon plasma’s equation of state (EOS), which is very precisely known from lattice QCD calculations
[
6
,
20
]
, we want to use first-principles theoretical input.

In this section we discuss how to consistently incorporate such direct information from QCD into a hydrodynamic framework that was originally derived from kinetic theory. We introduce a parametric model for an anisotropic equation of state that allows the anisotropic hydrodynamic equations, including the dissipative relaxation equations for the longitudinal pressure, transverse pressure and residual shear stresses, to be solved on a purely macroscopic level. This differs from earlier implementations of the framework which relied on the solution of evolution equations for the microscopic kinetic parameters
(\Lambda,\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L})

[
110
,
111
,
118
,
154
,
124
,
125
]
(which, for the case of QCD, are not really well-defined). However, since we will need these microscopic parameters for the calculation of those transport coefficients computed from kinetic theory (a temporary necessity that will disappear as soon as we find ways of calculating these transport coefficients directly from QCD), we determine them from the macroscopic hydrodynamic quantities, using our parametric model for the anisotropic EOS.
7
7

7

Note that the parametric model is not used for the QCD equilibrium pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})
but only to parametrize the dissipative deviations of the
longitudinal and transverse pressures from
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})
, as well as for the calculation of the remaining
transport coefficients.

To construct this parametric model we follow Refs.
[
7
,
154
,
170
]
and parametrize the response of the pressure anisotropy and the bulk viscous pressure to anisotropic expansion using a quasiparticle EOS. The quasiparticles have a temperature-dependent mass that is chosen such that a weakly-interacting gas of these particles accurately mimics the QCD EOS. The transport coefficients are then worked out in this kinetic theory.
8
8

8

Note that an accurate description of the QCD equation of state does not imply by any means that our
kinetic theory model also predicts the correct transport properties of the quark-gluon plasma.

It is well known
[
171
,
172
,
173
]
that for thermodynamic consistency such an approach requires the introduction of a mean-field
B
whose temperature dependence in equilibrium determines the temperature dependence of the quasiparticle’s effective mass
m(T)
. The mean-field also receives additional dissipative corrections out of equilibrium
[
170
]
.

3.3.1
Integrating the lattice QCD EOS with a quasiparticle EOS

The key question that needs to be addressed in anisotropic hydrodynamics is how much pressure anisotropy and bulk viscous pressure is generated by a given hydrodynamic expansion rate and its anisotropy. These are the two largest and most important dissipative effects in our approach. The answer to this question depends on the microscopic properties of the medium. For QCD matter this response is presently not known. It is, however, a key ingredient in the hydrodynamic evolution model. In this subsection we model this response by that of a weakly interacting gas of quasiparticles with a medium-dependent mass
m(T)
. Within this model we can associate (within certain limits) any given deviations of the longitudinal and transverse pressures
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
from the equilibrium pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})
with specific values for the microscopic parameters
\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha},m\bigl(T)\bigr)\bigr)
describing the anisotropic quasiparticle distribution function
f_{a}
. These values can then be used to compute the kinetic theory values for the transport coefficients. So while the equilibrium pressure is described by the lattice QCD EOS, the dissipative deviations of
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
from the equilibrium pressure are interpreted microscopically within a weakly interacting gas of massive Boltzmann particles. As we solve the hydrodynamic equations (
3.8
) – (
3.10
) together with the dissipative relaxation equations (
3.30
a-d), we interpret the resulting deviations from local equilibrium within the quasiparticle model by writing

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{(q)}-\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}\bigl(\mathcal{E}\bigr),

(3.33a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(q)}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}\bigl(\mathcal{E}\bigr),

(3.33b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(q)}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}\bigl(\mathcal{E}\bigr).

(3.33c)

Here the superscript
(q)
stands for “quasiparticle model”. The zero on the l.h.s. of the first of these equations reflects the Landau matching condition
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(T)
to the QCD energy density, which also provides us with the temperature
T
at which the quasiparticle mass
m(T)
and equilibrium mean-field
B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)
(see below) are evaluated. In the quasiparticle model the hydrodynamic quantities on the r.h.s. of (
3.33
) consist of kinetic and mean-field contributions
[
7
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{(k)}+B,

(3.34a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}-B,

(3.34b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}-B.

(3.34c)

The kinetic contributions are obtained from Eqs. (
3.21
b,c,d):

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(k)}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr)=\mathcal{I}_{2000}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr),

(3.35a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr)=\mathcal{I}_{2200}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr),

(3.35b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr)=\mathcal{I}_{2010}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr),

(3.35c)

where
T=T(\mathcal{E})
. The mean-field
B
consists of an equilibrium part
B_{\mathrm{eq}}
and a dissipative correction
\delta B
:

B=B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)+\delta B.

(3.36)

By Landau matching, the total quasiparticle energy density
\mathcal{E}^{(q)}
is fixed to its equilibrium value:

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)+B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T),

(3.37)

where
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)=\mathcal{I}_{2000}\bigl(T,\bm{1};m(T)\bigr)
. The Landau matching condition (
3.33
a) can then be rewritten as

\mathcal{I}_{2000}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha};m(T)\bigr)=\mathcal{I}_{2000}\bigl(T,\bm{1};m(T)\bigr)-\delta B.

(3.38)

This establishes a relation between the temperature and the kinetic theory parameters, provided that
\delta B
is determined. In the equilibrium limit, the quasiparticle pressure is

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}(T)-B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)\,,

(3.39)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}(T)=\mathcal{I}_{2200}\bigl(T,\bm{1};m(T)\bigr)
. The equilibrium terms in (
3.37
) and (
3.39
) are all functions of temperature.

For simplicity we assume that the quasiparticles have Boltzmann statistics
(\Theta=0)
. To ensure that at asymptotically high temperatures the equilibrium pressure and energy density of this Boltzmann gas approach the corresponding values of a quark-gluon gas with
2(N_{c}^{2}{-}1)
bosonic and
4N_{c}N_{f}
fermionic degrees of freedom, we normalize them by applying to the quasiparticle distribution function a degeneracy factor

g=\frac{\pi^{4}}{90}\Big[2(N_{c}^{2}{-}1)+\frac{7}{2}N_{c}N_{f}\Big]\,,

(3.40)

with
N_{c}=3
colors and
N_{f}=3
flavors, counting
u
,
d
, and
s
quarks only (heavier flavors are exponentially suppressed in the phenomenologically interesting temperature range and are therefore neglected). This degeneracy factor is part of the momentum integration measure
\int_{p}
in the definition (
3.18
).

Figure 3.1:
The normalized quasiparticle mass
z=m/T
(a), the derivative
dm/dT
(b) and the equilibrium mean-field
B_{\mathrm{eq}}/T^{4}
(c) obtained from Eqs. (
3.41
) – (
3.42
) using the lattice QCD EOS compiled by either the BEST Collaboration
[
5
]
(solid lines) or the Wuppertal-Budapest Collaboration
[
6
,
7
]
(dashed lines).

The thermal quasiparticle mass
m(T)
is chosen such that the equilibrium pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}(\mathcal{E})
and energy density
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}(\mathcal{E})
of the quasiparticle model agree with their lattice QCD counterparts. Technically this is done by expressing the lattice QCD entropy density
\mathcal{S}
in terms of the corresponding kinetic theory expression
\mathcal{S}^{(q)}
for a gas of quasiparticles with mass
m(T)
and Boltzmann statistics
[
7
]
:

\mathcal{S}=\frac{\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}}{T}=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}}{T}=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}}{T}=\frac{gT^{3}z^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}K_{3}(z),

(3.41)

where
K_{n}(z)
is the modified Bessel function with
z=m(T)/T
. For thermodynamic consistency the r.h.s. must satisfy
\mathcal{S}^{(q)}=d\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(q)}/dT
, which is ensured by setting

B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)=-\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\int^{T}_{0}dT^{\,\prime}\,(T^{\,\prime})^{3}z^{2}(T^{\,\prime})K_{1}(z(T^{\,\prime}))\,\frac{dm(T^{\prime})}{dT^{\prime}}\,.

(3.42)

Plots of the quasiparticle mass-to-temperature ratio
z
and equilibrium mean-field
B_{\mathrm{eq}}
as functions of
T
, using a 2010 lattice QCD EOS obtained by the Wuppertal-Budapest Collaboration
[
6
]
, can be found in Ref.
[
7
]
. In this chapter we use the QCD EOS compiled by the Beam Energy Scan Theory (BEST) Collaboration
[
5
]
. The resulting slightly modified temperature dependences of
z(T)
,
dm(T)/dT
and
B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)
are shown in Figure
3.1
as solid lines (together with the earlier results from Ref.
[
7
]
shown as dashed lines).

Equation (
3.42
) determines the mean-field in equilibrium. Out of equilibrium it receives a non-equilibrium correction
\delta B

[
170
]
. As shown in Ref.
[
170
]
, thermodynamic consistency and energy-momentum conservation can be used to derive from the Boltzmann equation the following general evolution equation for
B
:

\dot{B}+m\dot{m}\int_{p}f+\int_{p}(u\cdot p)\,C[f]=0.

(3.43)

By Landau matching, the non-equilibrium correction to the quasiparticle energy density
\mathcal{E}^{(q)}=\mathcal{E}^{(k)}+B
must vanish, hence

B=B_{\mathrm{eq}}-\int_{p}(u\cdot p)^{2}\delta f\,,

(3.44)

where
\delta f=f-f_{\mathrm{eq}}
. Substituting
f=f_{a}+\delta\tilde{f}
and using the relaxation time approximation

C[f]\approx-\frac{(u\cdot p)\,\delta f}{\tau_{r}}\,,

(3.45)

Eq. (
3.43
) takes the form

\dot{B}=\frac{B_{\mathrm{eq}}{-}B}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{\dot{m}}{m}\Big(\mathcal{E}^{(k)}{-}2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}{-}\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}\Big).

(3.46)

Note that the expression in the parentheses is the trace of the kinetic contribution to the energy-momentum tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
. Since the non-equilibrium component of the mean-field
\delta B=B-B_{\mathrm{eq}}
contributes to the bulk viscous pressure, we have replaced in Eq. (
3.46
) the relaxation time
\tau_{r}
by the bulk relaxation time
\tau_{\Pi}
. The time derivative of the thermal mass can be expressed in terms of the energy conservation law (
3.8
) using the chain rule (see footnote
5
).

Although Eq. (
3.44
) shows that the mean-field
B
is not an independent quantity, we find it most straightforward to use Eq. (
3.46
) to evolve it dynamically. It does not directly enter the evolution equations for the components of the energy-momentum tensor as an independent variable, but is only needed for the model interpretation of the pressure anisotropy and bulk viscous pressure (which are hydrodynamic outputs) in terms of the microscopic parameters
(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha},m)
needed for computing the kinetic transport coefficients. We use Eqs. (
3.8
), (
3.30
a), (
3.30
b) and (
3.46
) to evolve
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}
and
B
. The physical pressures are obtained from
\mathcal{P}_{L}=\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}-B
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}=\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}-B
. From
\mathcal{E}
we determine
T
using the lattice EOS, and thus we know
m(T)
. Then we rewrite our anisotropic equation of state model (
3.33
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}-B

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{2000}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha},m(T)\bigr),

(3.47a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}+B

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{2200}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha},m(T)\bigr),

(3.47b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\perp}+B

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{2010}\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha},m(T)\bigr),

(3.47c)

solve these equations for the anisotropy parameters
(\Lambda,\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L})
, and compute the transport coefficients. The numerical method which we use to solve the nonlinear equations (
3.47
) will be presented in the next chapter.

Of course, the values
\bigl(\Lambda,\bm{\alpha},m\bigr)
associated in this way with
\mathcal{P}_{L}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
and
\mathcal{E}
are model dependent. A different parametrization of the lattice QCD EOS in terms of quasiparticles (for example, as a mixture of different types of quasiparticles with different quantum statistical properties, degeneracy factors and masses) would yield different results.

With the anisotropic EOS model (
3.47
) we can finally write down the equations of motion for the total pressures
\mathcal{P}_{L}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
, by combining Eqs. (
3.30
a-b) with Eq. (
3.46
):

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{L}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}{-}\bar{\mathcal{P}}}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}{3\tau_{\pi}/2}+\bar{\zeta}^{L}_{z}\theta_{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{L}_{\perp}\theta_{\perp}-2W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\dot{z}_{\mu}+\bar{\lambda}^{L}_{Wu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}D_{z}u_{\mu}

(3.48)

\displaystyle+\,\bar{\lambda}^{L}_{W\perp}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}z_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}u^{\nu}-\bar{\lambda}^{L}_{\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp,{\mu\nu}},\qquad

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}{-}\bar{\mathcal{P}}}{\tau_{\Pi}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}{3\tau_{\pi}}+\bar{\zeta}^{\perp}_{z}\theta_{L}+\bar{\zeta}^{\perp}_{\perp}\theta_{\perp}+W^{\mu}_{\perp z}\dot{z}_{\mu}+\bar{\lambda}^{\perp}_{Wu}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}D_{z}u_{\mu}

(3.49)

\displaystyle\,-\bar{\lambda}^{\perp}_{W\perp}W^{\mu}_{\perp z}z_{\nu}\nabla_{\perp,\mu}u^{\nu}+\bar{\lambda}^{\perp}_{\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}\sigma_{\perp,{\mu\nu}}.

Here we redefined the transport coefficients for the longitudinal and transverse pressures as detailed in Chapter
4
. This completes our formalism for non-conformal anisotropic hydrodynamics, where the equations of motion (
3.8
) – (
3.10
), (
3.30
c), (
3.30
d), (
3.48
) and (
3.49
) are purely macroscopic and structurally independent of the underlying microscopic physics, while the transport coefficients (other than
\eta/\mathcal{S}
and
\zeta/\mathcal{S}
) are evaluated with our specific quasiparticle kinetic model for the anisotropic equation of state.

3.3.2
Initializing the mean-field and anisotropic parameters

In the anisotropic equation of state model (
3.47
) there is an ambiguity between the initialization of the mean-field
B
and of the kinetic terms
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}
,
\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}
. Standard hydrodynamic initial conditions for the energy-momentum tensor only provide
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{P}_{L}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
on the initialization hypersurface. The initial energy density profile
\mathcal{E}
also yields the initial temperature profile and thus the initial profile for the equilibrium part
B_{\mathrm{eq}}(T)
of the mean-field. Its co-moving time derivative can be obtained by taking the equilibrium limit of Eq. (
3.46
):

\dot{B}_{\mathrm{eq}}=-\frac{\dot{m}}{m}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}-3\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}^{(k)}\right).

(3.50)

To obtain a guess for the initial non-equilibrium deviation
\delta B
we assume that
\delta B
evolves on a time scale larger than the bulk viscous relaxation time
\tau_{\Pi}
. We can then ignore the time derivative of
\delta B
on the left hand side of Eq. (
3.46
) and obtain from the difference between Eqs. (
3.46
) and (
3.50
) the “asymptotic” initial condition

\delta B^{\mathrm{(asy)}}=\frac{3\tau_{\Pi}\dot{m}}{m-4\tau_{\Pi}\dot{m}}\Pi.

(3.51)

As before (see footnote
5
)
m
and
\dot{m}
can be expressed in terms of the energy density
\mathcal{E}
and its co-moving time derivative. Having thus specified the initial profile for the mean-field
B=B_{\mathrm{eq}}+\delta B
we can proceed to solve for the initial anisotropic parameters from Eq. (
3.47
) numerically and compute the initial values for the transport coefficients.

For far-from-equilibrium initial conditions, such as those provided by the IP-Glasma model
[
43
]
where
\mathcal{P}_{L}
starts out with a very large negative value
\mathcal{P}_{L}=-\mathcal{E}
and, after classical Yang-Mills evolution for a time of the order of the inverse saturation momentum, settles to around zero
[
174
,
175
]
, the implied deviation
\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}
can become so large that, with this initial choice of
B
, the quasiparticle model cannot accommodate it within the allowed ranges for
(\Lambda,\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L})
. Since typically
B<0
at high temperatures (see Fig.
3.1
), the kinetic longitudinal pressure,
\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}=\mathcal{P}_{L}+B
, may in this situation be negative. Specifically, the anisotropic parameter initialization for temperatures
T\gtrsim 0.5
GeV is found to fail when
\mathcal{P}_{L}/\mathcal{P}_{\perp}\lesssim 0.08
. To overcome this problem, in the case of such extreme initial conditions for
\mathcal{P}_{L}
one can simply adjust the initial guess for
\delta B
and increase the initial value for
B
by hand until a solution for
(\Lambda,\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L})
can be found. This is feasible for (0+1)–dimensional Bjorken flow (see next subsection) but much more difficult to do in (3+1)–dimensional anisotropic hydrodynamics. An alternative way of initializing the mean-field and anisotropic parameters for more realistic simulations of heavy-ion collision will be discussed in the next chapter.

3.4
Non-conformal Bjorken flow

In this section we test our anisotropic hydrodynamic formalism by comparing it to second-order viscous hydrodynamics for the case of (0+1)–dimensional Bjorken expansion in Milne spacetime
x^{\mu}=(\tau,x,y,\eta_{s})
, using the lattice QCD equation of state referenced in Fig.
3.1
. We begin by simplifying the anisotropic evolution equations (
3.8
) – (
3.10
), (
3.30
c-d), and (
3.48
) – (
3.49
) for systems with Bjorken symmetry (labeled “vahydro”). The fluid velocity is
u^{\mu}=(1,0,0,0)
, the longitudinal and transverse expansion rates are
\theta_{L}=1/\tau
and
\theta_{\perp}=0
, and the residual shear stress components
W^{\mu}_{\perp z}
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}_{\perp}
vanish by symmetry. As a result, the anisotropic hydrodynamic equations simplify to

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}=-\frac{\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{P}_{L}}{\tau},

(3.52a)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{L}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}{-}\bar{\mathcal{P}}}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}{3\tau_{\pi}/2}+\frac{\bar{\zeta}^{L}_{z}}{\tau},

(3.52b)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}{-}\bar{\mathcal{P}}}{\tau_{\Pi}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}{3\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{\bar{\zeta}^{\perp}_{z}}{\tau},

(3.52c)

\displaystyle\dot{B}=-\frac{B_{\mathrm{eq}}{-}B}{\tau_{\Pi}}+\frac{\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L}}{\tau m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\big(\mathcal{E}{-}2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}{-}\mathcal{P}_{L}{-}4B\big),

(3.52d)

where in Eq. (
3.52
d) we used

\frac{\dot{m}}{m}=-\frac{\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L}}{\tau m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\,,

(3.53)

as well as
\mathcal{E}^{(k)}-2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}-\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}=\mathcal{E}-2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}-\mathcal{P}_{L}-4B
.

In this chapter, we use a temperature dependent parametrization for the specific shear viscosity
\eta/\mathcal{S}

[
8
]
:

\eta/\mathcal{S}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\eta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{min}}+(\eta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{slope}}(T{-}T_{c})&\text{for}\ T>T_{c}\,,\\
(\eta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{min}}&\text{for}\ T\leq T_{c}\,,\end{array}\right.

(3.54)

where
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{E})
is the lattice QCD entropy density and
T_{c}=154
MeV is the pseudo-critical temperature. The model parameters
(\eta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{min}}=0.08
and
(\eta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{slope}}=0.85
GeV
-1
were extracted from an earlier global Bayesian analysis of LHC heavy-ion collision data
[
8
]
.
Similarly, we use for the specific bulk viscosity
\zeta/\mathcal{S}
the parameterization from Ref.
[
176
]
:

\zeta/\mathcal{S}=(\zeta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{norm}}\,w(T/T_{p})\,,

(3.55)

where the function
w(x)
is given by

w(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C_{1}+\lambda_{1}\exp\big[\frac{x-1}{\sigma_{1}}\big]+\lambda_{2}\exp\big[\frac{x-1}{\sigma_{2}}\big]&(x{\,<\,}0.995),\\
A_{0}+A_{1}x+A_{2}x^{2}&(0.995{\,\leq\,}x{\,\leq\,}1.05),\\
C_{2}+\lambda_{3}\exp\big[\frac{1-x}{\sigma_{3}}\big]+\lambda_{4}\exp\big[\frac{1-x}{\sigma_{4}}\big]&(x{\,>\,}1.05),\end{array}\right.

(3.56)

Figure 3.2:
The temperature dependence of (a) the specific shear viscosity
\eta/\mathcal{S}
, (b) the specific bulk viscosity
\zeta/\mathcal{S}
, and (c) the bulk viscosity
\zeta
itself (fm
-3
), given by the parameterizations (
3.54
) and (
3.55
)
[
8
]
. We note that the peak of
\zeta/\mathcal{S}
in (b) occurs at the temperature
T=0.995\,T_{c}\approx T_{c}
whereas the bulk viscosity
\zeta=(\zeta/\mathcal{S})\times\mathcal{S}
in panel (c) peaks at the higher temperature
T=1.05\,T_{c}
.

with
A_{0}=-13.45
,
A_{1}=27.55
,
A_{2}=-13.77
,
C_{1}=0.03
,
C_{2}=0.001
,
\lambda_{1}=0.9
,
\lambda_{2}=0.22
,
\lambda_{3}=0.9
,
\lambda_{4}=0.25
,
\sigma_{1}=0.0025
,
\sigma_{2}=0.022
,
\sigma_{3}=0.025
and
\sigma_{4}=0.13
. For the normalization factor we choose
(\zeta/\mathcal{S})_{\text{norm}}=1.25

[
8
]
, and we fix the location of the peak of the specific bulk viscosity by taking
T_{p}=T_{c}
. Figure
3.2
shows the behavior of the specific shear and bulk viscosities as a function of temperature (a different parametrization is used in the next chapter).

The relaxation times are then obtained from the kinetic theory relations (
3.32
), rewritten in the form

\tau_{\pi}=\frac{\eta}{\mathcal{S}}\,\frac{\mathcal{S}}{\beta_{\pi}},\qquad\tau_{\Pi}=\frac{\zeta}{\mathcal{S}}\,\frac{\mathcal{S}}{\beta_{\Pi}},

(3.57)

using the following quasiparticle versions of the
\beta
–coefficients
[
170
]
:

\beta_{\pi}=\frac{\mathcal{I}_{32}}{T}\,,\qquad\qquad\beta_{\Pi}=\frac{5\beta_{\pi}}{3}-c_{s}^{2}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})+c_{s}^{2}m\frac{dm}{dT}\mathcal{I}_{11}\,,

(3.58)

where the thermodynamic integrals are defined in Eq. (
B.36
). Here
c_{s}^{2}(\mathcal{E})
is the squared speed of sound from lattice QCD. The system of ordinary differential equations (
3.52
) is solved using Heun’s method. After each intermediate and full time step the anisotropic parameters are updated by numerically solving Eq. (
3.47
).

These anisotropic hydrodynamic results will be compared with those from second-order quasiparticle viscous hydrodynamics in the 14–moment approximation (labeled “vhydro”). The corresponding evolution equations and transport coefficients are derived in Appendix
B.4
. For Bjorken flow, the set of independent dynamical variables reduces to the energy density
\mathcal{E}
, the shear stress
\pi=-\tau^{2}\pi^{\eta\eta}=\frac{2}{3}(\mathcal{P}_{\perp}{-}\mathcal{P}_{L})
and the bulk viscous pressure
\Pi=\frac{1}{3}(2\mathcal{P}_{\perp}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L})-\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}
. Their evolution equations simplify to

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{E}}=-\frac{\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}+\Pi-\pi}{\tau},

(3.59a)

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}=-\frac{\pi}{\tau_{\pi}}-\frac{4\beta_{\pi}}{3\tau}-\frac{\left(\tau_{\pi\pi}{+}3\delta_{\pi\pi}\right)\pi-2\lambda_{\pi\Pi}\Pi}{3\tau_{\pi}\tau},

(3.59b)

\displaystyle\dot{\Pi}=-\frac{\Pi}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{\beta_{\Pi}}{\tau}{-}\frac{\delta_{\Pi\Pi}\Pi-\lambda_{\Pi\pi}\pi}{\tau_{\Pi}\tau}.

(3.59c)

For the non-equilibrium mean-field contribution
\delta B
we use the second-order expression
[
170
]

\delta B^{(2)}=-\frac{3\tau_{\Pi}}{m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})\,\Pi\,\theta\,,

(3.60)

where
\theta=\partial_{\mu}u^{\mu}=1/\tau
is the scalar expansion rate. In Eq. (
3.60
), we replaced the relaxation time
\tau_{r}
by
\tau_{\Pi}
. The relaxation times
\tau_{\pi}
and
\tau_{\Pi}
are obtained from Eqs. (
3.57
) – (
3.58
) while the second-order transport coefficients
\tau_{\pi\pi},\,\delta_{\pi\pi},\,\lambda_{\pi\Pi},\,\delta_{\Pi\Pi}
and
\lambda_{\Pi\pi}
are computed from the quasiparticle model in the 14–moment approximation (after expansion around a local-equilibrium distribution, see Appendix
B.4
). We will also look at how the transport coefficients, including the relaxation times, affect the viscous hydrodynamic results when computed in the small fixed mass approximation
z\ll 1
,
dm/dT\approx 0
and
B\approx 0
, which is commonly referred to as standard viscous hydrodynamics (labeled “vhydro 2”)
[
70
,
57
,
177
]
.

3.4.1
Equilibrium initial conditions

Figure 3.3:
