\displaystyle=-\frac{\tau_{r}}{3!}\left((\tau^{(1)}_{r}\big)^{2}+\tau_{r}\tau^{(2)}_{r}\right)\,,

(8.20d)

where
\tau^{(n)}_{r}\equiv\partial^{n}_{\tau}\tau_{r}(\tau)
; they satisfy the recurrence relation

\displaystyle c_{0}

\displaystyle=\tau\,,

(8.21a)

\displaystyle c_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tau_{r}\partial_{\tau}c_{n{-}1}}{n}\indent\forall\,n\geq 1\,.

(8.21b)

With these coefficients, we can now evaluate the integral (
8.16
) after Taylor expanding the integrand:

f_{\text{G}}(\tau,p)=\int^{z_{0}}_{0}dz\,e^{-z}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\big(h^{-1}(z,\tau)-\tau\big)^{n}f_{\text{eq}}^{(n)}(\tau,p)}{n!}\,.

(8.22)

As a demonstration, we compute the series up to
n=3
and truncate the expression at third order in derivatives:

\begin{split}f_{\text{G}}\approx&\,(1-e^{-z_{0}})f_{\text{eq}}\,+\,\big(1-\Gamma(2,z_{0})\big)\delta f^{(1)}\,+\,\\
&\Big(1-\frac{\Gamma(3,z_{0})}{2!}\Big)\delta f^{(2)}\,+\,\Big(1-\frac{\Gamma(4,z_{0})}{3!}\Big)\delta f^{(3)}\,,\end{split}

(8.23)

where
\Gamma(n{+}1,z_{0})=\int^{\infty}_{z_{0}}dz\,e^{-z}z^{n}
are the upper incomplete gamma functions. After taking the limit
z_{0}\to\infty
, Eq. (
8.23
) reduces to

f_{\text{G}}\approx f_{\text{eq}}+\delta f^{(1)}+\delta f^{(2)}+\delta f^{(3)}\,,

(8.24)

where

\displaystyle\delta f^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\,-\tau_{r}f_{\text{eq}}^{(1)}\,,

(8.25a)

\displaystyle\delta f^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\ \tau_{r}\tau_{r}^{(1)}f_{\text{eq}}^{(1)}\,+\,\tau_{r}^{2}f_{\text{eq}}^{(2)}\,,

(8.25b)

\displaystyle\delta f^{(3)}=

\displaystyle-\tau_{r}\big(\tau_{r}^{(1)}\big)^{2}f_{\text{eq}}^{(1)}\,-\,\tau_{r}^{2}\tau_{r}^{(2)}f_{\text{eq}}^{(1)}-\,3\tau_{r}^{2}\tau_{r}^{(1)}f_{\text{eq}}^{(2)}\,-\,\tau_{r}^{3}f_{\text{eq}}^{(3)}\,.

(8.25c)

These are precisely the non-equilibrium corrections in the Chapman-Enskog series (
8.9
). Using a computer-generated code
8
8

8

The codes used in this chapter can be downloaded at
https://github.com/mjmcnelis/rta_resum
.

we verified that the series (
8.23
) works up to order
O(\text{Kn}^{40}
):

\begin{split}f_{\text{G}}(\tau,p)&\approx{\int_{0}^{z_{0}}}dz\,e^{-z}\sum_{n=0}^{40}\frac{z^{n}{\left[-\tau_{r}(\tau)\partial_{\tau}\right]^{n}}f_{\text{eq}}(\tau,p)}{n!}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{40}\left(1-\frac{\Gamma(n{+}1,z_{0})}{n!}\right){\left[-\tau_{r}(\tau)\partial_{\tau}\right]^{n}}f_{\text{eq}}(\tau,p)\,.\end{split}

(8.26)

A proof that the (0+1)–d hydrodynamic generator (
8.14
) maps to the Borel resummed RTA Chapman–Enskog series (
8.10
) at all orders in the Knudsen number as
z_{0}\to\infty
has been generously provided by Chandrodoy Chattopadhyay in Appendix
E.1
.

8.2
Series expansion of the hydrodynamic generator

In the limit of vanishing non-hydrodynamic modes, the hydrodynamic generator (
8.14
) provides a shortcut to the Borel resummation (
8.10
) without needing to compute it directly. It also satisfies the RTA Boltzmann equation

\begin{split}\partial_{\tau}f_{\text{G}}(\tau,p)=&\,\frac{f_{\text{eq}}(\tau,p)}{\tau_{r}(\tau)}-\int^{\tau}_{\tau_{0}}\frac{d\tau^{\prime}D(\tau,\tau^{\prime})f_{\text{eq}}(\tau^{\prime},p)}{\tau_{r}(\tau)\tau_{r}(\tau^{\prime})}=\,\frac{f_{\text{eq}}(\tau,p)-f_{\text{G}}(\tau,p)}{\tau_{r}(\tau)}\,,\end{split}

(8.27)

where we used the identities
\partial_{\tau}D(\tau,\tau^{\prime}){\,=\,}-D(\tau,\tau^{\prime})/\tau_{r}(\tau)
and
D(\tau,\tau){\,=\,}1
. However, this alone does not tell us how much hydrodynamics contributes to the dynamics of the system at finite times, before the initial state
f_{0}(\tau_{0},p)
has completely decayed. As long as the non-hydrodynamic modes contribute, the expansion of the exact distribution function

f(\tau,p)=e^{-z_{0}}f_{0}(\tau_{0},p)+f_{\text{G}}(\tau,p)

(8.28)

around local equilibrium looks like
9
9

9

This expansion retains the same transseries-like structure for both early and late times. Transasymptotic solutions for the moments of the distribution function have been studied for Bjorken expansion and have been found to accurately reproduce the numerical solution of the moments equations even when continued back to earlier times
[
216
,
217
]
.

f=f_{\text{eq}}+\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(0)}+\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(1)}+\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(2)}+\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(3)}+O(\text{Kn}^{4})\,,

(8.29)

where

\displaystyle\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=e^{-z_{0}}\left(f_{0}-f_{\text{eq}}\right)\,,

(8.30a)

\displaystyle\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{\Gamma(n{+}1,z_{0})}{n!}\right)\delta f^{(n)}\indent\forall\,n\geq 1\,.

(8.30b)

The zeroth-order correction
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(0)}
, which combines the initial-state term with the first term in Eq. (
8.23
), is a purely non-hydrodynamic mode and is only present for a short period of time
\sim\tau_{r}
. The other
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(n)}
corrections are the usual hydrodynamic gradient corrections, except they are initially suppressed by their associated non-hydrodynamic mode. These non-perturbative factors control the emerging strengths of the gradient corrections to the distribution function as the particle interactions drive the system towards hydrodynamics over time (i.e. as
z_{0}
increases). In particular, as will be discussed below, higher-order gradient corrections are suppressed more strongly and for a longer duration than the lower-order terms.

To study these new non-perturbative effects on the hydrodynamic gradient expansion, we evolve a conformal fluid undergoing Bjorken expansion with the exact solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation
[
101
,
159
,
218
]
. We initialize the system at
\tau_{0}=0.25
fm/
c
with initial temperature
T(\tau_{0})=0.6
GeV and shear stress
\pi_{s}(\tau_{0})=0
, where
\pi_{s}\equiv\frac{2}{3}(\mathcal{P}_{\perp}{-}\mathcal{P}_{L})
(by definition
\pi_{s,\mathrm{eq}}=0
). For the relaxation time we take
\tau_{r}=\tau_{\pi}
with
\tau_{\pi}T=5(\eta/\mathcal{S})
and set the specific shear viscosity to
\eta/\mathcal{S}=3/(4\pi)
. Using these initial conditions we construct the temperature
T(\tau)
by fixing the exact solution (
8.8
) to the Landau matching condition
\mathcal{E}(\tau)=3T^{4}(\tau)/\pi^{2}
or
[
101
]

\begin{split}T^{4}(\tau)=&\,D(\tau,\tau_{0})\,T^{4}(\tau_{0})\,\mathcal{H}\left(\frac{\tau_{0}}{\tau}\right)+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\frac{d\tau^{\prime}}{\tau_{\pi}({\tau^{\prime}})}D(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\,T^{4}(\tau^{\prime})\,\mathcal{H}\left(\dfrac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}\right)\,,\end{split}

(8.31)

where

\mathcal{H}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\left(x^{2}+\frac{{\tan^{-1}}\sqrt{x^{-2}-1}}{\sqrt{x^{-2}-1}}\right)\,.

(8.32)

The most straightforward way to solve this integral equation is by using fixed-point iteration. After computing the temperature, we evaluate the normalized shear stress
10
10

10

This differs from the traditional definition
\bar{\pi}\equiv\pi/(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})
which reduces to
\bar{\pi}=\pi/(4\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})
in the conformal limit.

\bar{\pi}_{s}(\tau)=\pi_{s}(\tau)/\mathcal{P}_{\text{eq}}(\tau)
, where
\mathcal{P}_{\text{eq}}(\tau)=\mathcal{E}(\tau)/3
is the equilibrium pressure
[
101
]
:

\begin{split}\bar{\pi}_{s}(\tau)=\,&D(\tau,\tau_{0})\frac{T^{4}(\tau_{0})}{T^{4}(\tau)}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\perp}\Big(\frac{\tau_{0}}{\tau}\Big)-\mathcal{H}_{L}\Big(\frac{\tau_{0}}{\tau}\Big)\right]\\
&+\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\frac{d\tau^{\prime}}{\tau_{\pi}({\tau^{\prime}})}D(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\frac{T^{4}(\tau^{\prime})}{T^{4}(\tau)}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\perp}\Big(\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}\Big)-\mathcal{H}_{L}\Big(\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\tau}\Big)\right]\,.\end{split}

(8.33)

Figure 8.1:

The evolution of the exact pressure anisotropy
\bar{\pi}_{s}(\tau)
(solid black) for conformal Bjorken expansion. We plot the contributions of the
\delta f_{\text{G}}
corrections (dashed color) to the exact solution and compare them to Navier–Stokes (solid blue), Burnett (solid red), super-Burnett (solid green) and DNMR (solid purple) viscous hydrodynamics.

The functions
\mathcal{H}_{\perp}
and
\mathcal{H}_{L}
are defined in Appendix
E.3
.

The resulting exact evolution of the normalized shear stress is shown as the solid black line in Figure
8.1
. This exact solution is compared with various approximations discussed below. With the exact temperature and shear stress at hand, we evaluate and plot the contributions to the pressure anisotropy from the
\delta f_{\text{G}}
corrections up to third order (see Appendices
E.2
and
E.3
):

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s,\text{G}}^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\,e^{-z_{0}}\frac{T^{4}_{0}}{T^{4}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{\perp}\Big(\frac{\tau_{0}}{\tau}\Big)-\mathcal{H}_{L}\Big(\frac{\tau_{0}}{\tau}\Big)\right]\,,

(8.34a)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s,\text{G}}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\,\left(1-\Gamma(2,z_{0})\right)\frac{16\tau_{\pi}}{15\tau}\,,

(8.34b)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s,\text{G}}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\,\left(1-\frac{\Gamma(3,z_{0})}{2!}\right)\frac{-16\tau_{\pi}^{2}}{105\tau^{2}}\,\bigl(15+49\tau\partial_{\tau}{\ln T}\bigr),

(8.34c)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s,\text{G}}^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\,\left(1-\frac{\Gamma(4,z_{0})}{3!}\right)\frac{16\tau_{\pi}^{3}}{105\tau^{3}}\bigl(\tau\partial_{\tau}{\ln T}(135{+}182\tau\partial_{\tau}{\ln T})+77\tau^{2}\partial^{2}_{\tau}{\ln T}\bigr)\,.

(8.34d)

Here the energy conservation law and its time derivative

\displaystyle\tau\partial_{\tau}{\ln T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\pi}_{s}-4}{12}\,,

(8.35a)

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\partial^{2}_{\tau}{\ln T}

\displaystyle=\frac{4-\bar{\pi}_{s}+\tau\partial_{\tau}\bar{\pi}_{s}}{12}

(8.35b)

are evaluated numerically using the exact solution. We further compare these
\delta f_{\text{G}}
corrections to
\bar{\pi}_{s}
to the first-order Navier–Stokes, second-order Burnett and third-order super-Burnett solutions,

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{NS})}

\displaystyle=\frac{16\tau_{\pi}}{15\tau}\,,

(8.36a)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{B})}

\displaystyle=\frac{16\tau_{\pi}}{15\tau}+\frac{64\tau_{\pi}^{2}}{315\tau^{2}}\,,

(8.36b)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{SB})}

\displaystyle=\frac{16\tau_{\pi}}{15\tau}+\frac{64\tau_{\pi}^{2}}{315\tau^{2}}-\frac{832\tau_{\pi}^{3}}{1575\tau^{3}}\,,

(8.36c)

as well as to the numerical solution of the standard viscous hydrodynamic equations (DNMR)
[
55
,
149
]
:

\displaystyle\tau\partial_{\tau}{\ln T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\pi}_{s}-4}{12}\,,

(8.37a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\bar{\pi}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\bar{\pi}_{s}}{\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{16}{15\tau}-\frac{10\bar{\pi}_{s}}{21\tau}-\frac{\bar{\pi}_{s}^{2}}{3\tau}\,.

(8.37b)

At early times, the non-hydrodynamic mode
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(0)}
dominates the evolution of the pressure anisotropy and is responsible for the initial rise away from the local equilibrium initial condition
\bar{\pi}_{s}(\tau_{0})=0
(see Figure
8.1
). As the system hydrodynamizes,
11
11

11

We define hydrodynamization as the time when the leading non-hydrodynamic mode
\bar{\pi}_{\text{G}}^{(0)}
decays to 10% of its maximum value. In Fig.
8.1
this occurs at
\tau=2.47
fm/
c
(or
z_{0}=3.6
).

the initial-state function decays and the first-order gradient correction
\delta f^{(1)}_{\text{G}}
emerges as the leading correction to the local-equilibrium distribution
f_{\text{eq}}
in Eq. (
8.29
). Already, we see that the addition of
\delta f^{(1)}_{\text{G}}
nearly captures the exact pressure anisotropy. This is in stark contrast to the Navier–Stokes solution, which misses both
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(0)}
in (
8.34
a) and the prefactor
1-\Gamma(2,z_{0})
in (
8.34
b) and hence fails to reproduce the shear stress for
\tau\lesssim 2
fm/
c
. The reader should also take note of the similarity between the blue-dashed curve and DNMR viscous hydrodynamics, where the
\delta f
correction used to compute the transport coefficients of the relaxation equation (
8.37
b) is first-order in the shear stress.

Compared to the second-order correction accounted for in the Burnett solution (
8.36
b), the full
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(2)}
gradient correction to the shear stress is much weaker at early times since it is strongly suppressed by the corresponding non-hydrodynamic mode. By the time the non-perturbative factor
1-\frac{1}{2!}\Gamma(3,z_{0})
has decayed by 90% (at around
\tau=3.9
fm/
c
), the gradients characterized by the Knudsen number
\text{Kn}=\tau_{\pi}/\tau\approx 0.2
have already greatly diminished. As a result, the
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(2)}
correction ends up having little overall impact on the evolution of the system. A similar observation holds for the third-order correction
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(3)}
. The combined low-order
\delta f_{\text{G}}
corrections to the local-equilibrium distribution are seen to provide excellent agreement with the exact solution. While we caution the reader that this does not necessarily mean the rest of the series (
8.29
) will converge, take this observation as justification to truncate the new expansion scheme (
8.29
) at a low order: Figure
8.1
makes it clear that, at least for Bjorken flow, gradient corrections beyond first order have almost negligible influence on the fluid’s dynamics during the early stages of evolution even though there the expansion rate is large. We conclude that the non-perturbative corrections in Eq. (
8.29
) are essential ingredients in constructing an accurate solution for the RTA Boltzmann equation, along with the usual Chapman–Enskog expansion.

8.3
Applications for conformal Bjorken flow

8.3.1
Far-from-equilibrium hydrodynamics

The study from the previous section showed that only the initial-state term
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(0)}
and first-order generator correction
\delta f_{\text{G}}^{(1)}
are needed to capture most of the shear stress (at least for Bjorken expansion). Therefore, we can apply our new expansion scheme (
8.29
) to derive hydrodynamic equations for a conformal gas subject to Bjorken flow that are just as accurate as anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching.

In conformal Bjorken expansion, there are only two hydrodynamic degrees of freedom: the energy density
\mathcal{E}
and the shear stress
\pi_{s}
. Their kinetic definitions are

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{p}\frac{v^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,f(\tau,p_{\perp},w)\,,

(8.38a)

\displaystyle\pi_{s}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\int_{p}\left[\frac{1}{2}p_{\perp}^{2}-\frac{w^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\right]\delta f(\tau,p_{\perp},w)\,,

(8.38b)

where
w=\tau^{2}p^{\eta}
,
v=\sqrt{\tau^{2}p_{\perp}^{2}+w^{2}}
,
\int_{p}=\int\dfrac{d^{2}p_{\perp}dw}{v(2\pi)^{3}}
is the momentum integration measure and
\delta f=f-f_{\text{eq}}
(we excluded the term
f_{\text{eq}}
in the integrand of Eq. (
8.38
b) since it vanishes by symmetry). The evolution equation for
\mathcal{E}
is given by the energy conservation law (
8.35
), rewritten as

\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{E}=\frac{3\pi_{s}-4\mathcal{E}}{3\tau}\,,

(8.39)

where we used the conformal relation
\mathcal{E}=3T^{4}/\pi^{2}
. For the shear relaxation equation, we follow Denicol’s method
[
219
,
55
]
by taking the time derivative of Eq. (
8.38
b). After expanding the r.h.s. of Eq. (
8.38
b) and substituting
\partial_{\tau}\delta f
from the RTA Boltzmann equation (
8.4
)

\partial_{\tau}\delta f=-\frac{\delta f}{\tau_{\pi}}-\partial_{\tau}f_{\text{eq}}\,,

(8.40)

the relaxation equation for the shear stress is
12
12

12

In Eq. (
8.41
), there is an additional term
\propto\delta\mathcal{E}
(see Eq. (
8.43
)), which vanishes after Landau matching.

\partial_{\tau}\pi_{s}=-\frac{\pi_{s}}{\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{16\mathcal{E}}{45\tau}+\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{p}\left[\frac{8w^{2}}{3\tau^{2}}-\frac{w^{4}}{v^{2}\tau^{2}}\right]\delta f\,.

(8.41)

To close the system of equations (
8.39
) and (
8.41
), we need some approximation for
\delta f
, for which we take the first two terms of the expansion (
8.29
):

\delta f=c_{0}(f_{0}{-}f_{\text{eq}})+c_{1}\delta f^{(1)}\,.

(8.42)

Here we replaced the non-perturbative factors with two coefficients
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
, which are adjusted to match the energy density and shear stress. We solve for the coefficients by inserting Eq. (
8.42
) into the Landau matching condition for the energy density,

\delta\mathcal{E}=\int_{p}\frac{v^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,\delta f=0\,,

(8.43)

and into the kinetic shear stress (
8.38
b). After decoupling the algebraic equations one obtains

c_{0}=\frac{\pi^{2}_{s}}{\pi_{s}^{(f_{0})}\pi_{s}+\frac{16}{45}(\mathcal{E}^{(f_{0})}{-}\mathcal{E})\mathcal{E}}\,,\qquad c_{1}=\frac{\tau}{\tau_{\pi}}\times\frac{\pi_{s}(\mathcal{E}^{(f_{0})}{-}\mathcal{E})}{\pi_{s}^{(f_{0})}\pi_{s}+\frac{16}{45}(\mathcal{E}^{(f_{0})}{-}\mathcal{E})\mathcal{E}}\,,

(8.44)

where

\mathcal{E}^{(f_{0})}=\int_{p}\frac{v^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,f_{0}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)\,,\qquad\pi_{s}^{(f_{0})}=\frac{2}{3}\int_{p}\left[\frac{1}{2}p_{\perp}^{2}-\frac{w^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\right]f_{0}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)\,,

(8.45)

Initially, the coefficients are
c_{0}(\tau_{0})=1
and
c_{1}(\tau_{0})=0
, while at late times we find that they qualitatively follow the evolution of the non-perturbative factors
e^{-z_{0}}
and
1-\Gamma(2,z_{0})
(i.e.
c_{0}\to 0
and
c_{1}\to 1
).

With the expansion coefficients computed, we can finally write down the final relaxation equation for the shear stress:

\partial_{\tau}{\pi_{s}}_{|\text{FFE}}=-\frac{\pi_{s}}{\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{16\mathcal{E}}{45\tau}+\frac{\zeta_{\pi}}{\tau}\,,

(8.46)

which we label as “far-from-equilibrium” (FFE) hydrodynamics. The shear transport coefficient
\zeta_{\pi}
is

\zeta_{\pi}=c_{0}\Big(\frac{8}{3}\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(f_{0})}-\mathcal{I}_{240}^{(f_{0})}-\frac{31}{45}\mathcal{E}\Big)\,-\,\frac{c_{1}\tau_{\pi}}{945\tau}\left(608\mathcal{E}+651\pi_{s}\right)\,,

(8.47)

where

\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(f_{0})}=\int_{p}\frac{w^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,f_{0}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)\,,\qquad\mathcal{I}_{240}^{(f_{0})}=\int_{p}\frac{w^{4}}{v^{2}\tau^{2}}\,f_{0}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)\,.

(8.48)

Although the FFE hydrodynamic equations (
8.39
) and (
8.46
) do not make any reference to the exact solution (
8.8
), we still need to specify the initial distribution
f_{0}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)
in Eq. (
8.42
). This means that the macroscopic evolution equations require some microscopic input, which is unavoidable since we are taking directly into account the evolution of non-hydrodynamic modes. For the following example, we simply use the leading-order anisotropic distribution

f_{0}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)=f_{a}(\tau_{0},p_{\perp},w)=\exp\left[-\frac{\sqrt{\tau_{0}^{2}p_{\perp}^{2}+(1{+}\xi_{0})w^{2}}}{\tau_{0}\Lambda_{0}}\right]\,,

(8.49)

where the initial effective temperature and anisotropy parameter (
\Lambda_{0}
,
\xi_{0}
) can vary depending on the initial conditions. However, it is often the case that one only has information about the macroscopic energy-momentum tensor
T^{\mu\nu}_{0}
(and charged currents
J^{\mu}_{0}
if any) on an initial-state hypersurface. For these situations, one could set the initial
\delta f
correction to the 14–moment approximation while initializing higher moments of the distribution function as zero.

We will compare Eq. (
8.46
) to DNMR second-order viscous hydrodynamics
[
55
,
149
]
, Jaiswal’s third-order viscous hydrodynamics
[
220
]
and anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching
[
118
]
:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\pi_{s}}_{|\text{DNMR}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi_{s}}{\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{16\mathcal{E}}{45\tau}-\frac{38\pi_{s}}{21\tau}\,,

(8.50a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\pi_{s}}_{|\text{Jaiswal}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi_{s}}{\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{16\mathcal{E}}{45\tau}-\frac{38\pi_{s}}{21\tau}-\frac{54\pi_{s}^{2}}{49\mathcal{E}\tau}\,,

(8.50b)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}{\pi_{s}}_{|\text{AH}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi_{s}}{\tau_{\pi}}+\frac{16\mathcal{E}}{45\tau}+\frac{\frac{8}{3}\mathcal{P}_{L}-\mathcal{I}_{240}^{(a)}-\frac{31}{45}\mathcal{E}}{\tau}\,,

(8.50c)

where

\mathcal{I}_{240}^{(a)}=\int_{p}\frac{w^{4}}{v^{2}\tau^{2}}\,f_{a}(\tau,p_{\perp},w)

(8.51)

and we have rewritten Eq. (
4.92
b) in terms of
\pi_{s}
using the conformal relation
\mathcal{P}_{L}=\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{E}-\pi_{s}
. We note that the transport coefficient
\zeta_{\pi}
in Eq. (
8.47
) has essentially the same structure as the
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching coefficient at the initial time
\tau_{0}
. In the near-equilibrium limit at late times
\pi_{s}/\mathcal{E}\approx 16\tau_{\pi}/(45\tau)\ll 1
,
\zeta_{\pi}\to-38\pi_{s}/21
, which is the DNMR coefficient in Eq. (
8.50
a).

In Figure
8.2
a we plot the normalized shear stress evolution given by the four hydrodynamic models against the exact conformal RTA solution (
8.33
). Compared to Fig.
8.1
, we start at equilibrium (
\bar{\pi}_{s}(\tau_{0})=0
) but use more extreme initial conditions: we set the initial time to
\tau_{0}=0.1
fm/
c
, the initial temperature to
T_{0}=0.75
GeV and use a larger shear viscosity
\eta/\mathcal{S}=10/(4\pi)
. By driving the system farther from equilibrium, we can better distinguish the hydrodynamic models. We see that DNMR is qualitatively correct but it overshoots the peak of the exact solution at
\tau\sim 1
fm/
c
. Jaiswal’s third-order method performs better than DNMR hydrodynamics but is still about 5% below the peak. In contrast, we see that FFE hydrodynamics and the
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching scheme almost exactly reproduce the RTA solution. The two approaches also yield nearly identical results in Fig.
8.2
b, where we start far from equilibrium (
\bar{\pi}_{s}(\tau_{0})\approx 1
). This indicates a strong connection between anisotropic hydrodynamics and the resummed expansion technique (
8.29
).

Figure 8.2:

Conformal Bjorken evolution of the normalized shear stress with equilibrium (a) and longitudinally free-streaming (b) initial conditions. We compare the results from DNMR second-order viscous hydrodynamics (dashed purple), Jaiswal’s third-order viscous hydrodynamics (dashed green), anisotropic hydrodynamics with
\mathcal{P}_{L}
–matching (dashed red) and FFE hydrodynamics (dashed blue) to the exact RTA solution (solid black).

The study here shows that for a hydrodynamic model to make precise predictions, including the initial state’s non-equilibrium dynamics directly in the transport equations is essential. By considering microscopic input in the
\delta f
expansion (
8.42
), the FFE approach reliably captures the exact hydrodynamic evolution. We note that FFE hydrodynamics is similar to another expansion scheme recently proposed by Alwawi and Strickland
[
221
]
; their ansatz for the
\delta f
correction is

\delta f=e^{-z_{0}}(f_{0}-f_{\text{eq}})\,+\,(1-e^{-z_{0}})(f_{a}-f_{\text{eq}})\,.

(8.52)

Compared to Eq. (
8.42
), they use strictly exponential coefficients and replace the first-order Chapman–Enskog correction
\delta f^{(1)}
by the non-equilibrium part of the anisotropic distribution. Their approach
[
221
]
turns out to be more effective than ordinary anisotropic hydrodynamics. Thus, optimizing the expansion schemes (
8.42
) and (
8.52
) and generalizing them to non-conformal fluids would be worth exploring in future research.

Figure 8.3:

Conformal RTA attractor of the shear inverse Reynolds number as a function of the inverse Knudsen number
\text{Kn}^{-1}=w
(solid purple). We also plot the contributions from the initial-state term (solid orange) and hydrodynamic generator (solid blue). Our approximations for the total attractor and its constituents are given by the dashed-color curves.

8.3.2
Hydrodynamic attractor

We can also use the hydrodynamic generator expansion to construct the hydrodynamic attractor
[
95
]
, specifically the conformal RTA attractor of the normalized shear stress
\bar{\pi}_{s}
. Numerically, the RTA attractor can be extracted by evolving the exact solution (
8.8
) whose initial conditions satisfy the slow-roll approximation
[
98
]

\frac{d\bar{\pi}_{s}}{dw}_{\big|w=w_{0}}=0\,,

(8.53)

where
w=\tau/\tau_{\pi}
and we set the initial value to
w_{0}=10^{-3}
. For an initial anisotropic distribution (
8.49
), the anisotropy parameter is tuned to
\alpha_{L,0}=(1\,{+}\,\xi_{0})^{-1/2}=0.013
, which corresponds to the longitudinal free-streaming limit
\bar{\pi}_{s}(w_{0})\approx 1
or
\mathcal{P}_{L}(w_{0})\approx 0
. Figure
8.3
shows the exact RTA attractor as a function of
w
(solid purple). We see that the attractor remains finite as the Knusden number
\text{Kn}=1/w
increases (i.e.
w\to 0
) instead of diverging like the Navier–Stokes solution in Eq. (
8.36
a) (rewritten as
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{NS})}=16/(15w)
).

We approximate the hydrodynamic attractor by first decomposing it as

\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{A})}(w)=\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}(w)+\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}(w)\,,

(8.54)

where
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}
and
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}
are the first and second terms of the exact shear stress (
8.33
), respectively. The two contributions are plotted in Fig.
8.3
; as expected, the initial-state term dominates the attractor for small values of
w
while the generator term takes over at large
w
. Our strategy is to model these two curves as a function of
w
by applying our expansion scheme (
E.38
) and (
8.34
b-d) for a system initialized with
\bar{\pi}_{s}(w_{0})=1
at
w_{0}\to 0
.

We start by taking the initial-state and first-order generator corrections from Eqs. (
E.38
) and (
8.34
b):

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=e^{-z_{0}}\,\left(\frac{T_{0}}{T}\right)^{4}\times\frac{\mathcal{H}_{\perp}{\left(\dfrac{w_{0}\alpha_{L,0}T}{wT_{0}}\right)}-2\mathcal{H}_{L}{\left(\dfrac{w_{0}\alpha_{L,0}T}{wT_{0}}\right)}}{2\mathcal{H}(\alpha_{L,0})}\,,

(8.55a)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\Gamma\left[2,z_{0}\right]\right)\frac{16}{15w}\,.

(8.55b)

In the first line, we replaced
\Lambda_{0}
by Eq. (
E.36
) and used the relation
\tau_{0}/\tau=w_{0}T/(wT_{0})
. After taking the limit
\alpha_{L,0}\to 0
, Eq. (
8.55
a) reduces to

\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}=e^{-z_{0}}\,\frac{w_{0}}{w}\left(\frac{T_{0}}{T}\right)^{3}\,.

(8.56)

The temperature’s evolution is given by the energy conservation law (
8.35
), rewritten as

w\frac{d\ln T}{dw}=\frac{\bar{\pi}_{s}-4}{8+\bar{\pi}_{s}}\,,

(8.57)

where we used the chain rule identity
12\tau\frac{d}{d\tau}=w(8+\bar{\pi}_{s})\frac{d}{dw}
. About
w=0
, the longitudinal pressure is approximately zero (i.e.
\bar{\pi}_{s}\approx 1
); thus, the temperature initially follows the free-streaming solution

T\approx T^{(0)}=T_{0}\left(\frac{w_{0}}{w}\right)^{1/3}\,.

(8.58)

As a result, the factors in Eq. (
8.56
) cancel at zeroth order and we can safely take the limit
w_{0}\to 0
:

\lim_{w_{0}\to\,0}\,\frac{w_{0}}{w}\left(\frac{T_{0}}{T}\right)^{3}=1+O(w)\,.

(8.59)

The first-order expression for the particle interaction measure
z_{0}
is

z_{0}=\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\frac{d\tau^{\prime}}{\tau_{\pi}(\tau^{\prime})}=\int_{w_{0}}^{w}\frac{12\,dw^{\prime}}{8+\bar{\pi}_{s}(w^{\prime})}\approx z_{0}^{(1)}=\frac{4w}{3}\,.

(8.60)

Our first trial solution (not shown in Fig.
8.3
) then simplifies to

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=e^{-z_{0}^{(1)}}\,,

(8.61a)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\Gamma\big[2,z_{0}^{(1)}\big]\right)\frac{16}{15w}\,.

(8.61b)

The combined expression works well for small and large values of
w
but not for intermediate values. Before considering the higher-order generator corrections, we make a first-order correction to the temperature evolution in Eq. (
8.59
) via perturbation. After inserting the trial solution
\bar{\pi}_{s}=\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}+\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}
from Eq. (
8.61
) into Eq. (
8.57
) and solving the differential equation to first order in
w
, one obtains

T\approx T^{(1)}=T_{0}\left(\frac{w_{0}}{w}\right)^{1/3}\left(1-\frac{208w}{3645}\right)\,.

(8.62)

Thus, our approximation for the initial-state term becomes

\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}=e^{-z_{0}^{(1)}}\left(1+\frac{208w}{1215}\right)\,,

(8.63)

which is given by the orange-dashed curve in Fig.
8.3
. One sees that it captures the exact
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}
term very well even for intermediate values of
w\sim 0.1-1
. Although this solution breaks down for large
w
, the errors relative to the total attractor in that region are exponentially small.

Now we can finally turn our attention to the higher-order generator corrections (
8.34
c-d), which will further close the gap at intermediate values of
w
. We solve for the second-order generator correction perturbatively by inserting our refined trial solution (
8.63
) and (
8.61
b) into Eq. (
8.34
c):

\bar{\pi}_{s,\text{G}}^{(2)}=\Bigg(1-\frac{\Gamma\big[3,z_{0}^{(1)}\big]}{2!}\Bigg)\frac{16}{105w^{2}}\,\left[\frac{4}{3}-\frac{49\big(\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(0)}{+}\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}\big)}{12}\right]\,.

(8.64)

Then we add this correction to our current approximation for
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}
in Eq. (
8.61
b). We repeat this procedure for the third-order generator correction
\bar{\pi}_{s,\text{G}}^{(3)}
in Eq. (
8.34
d), but the expression is too cumbersome to show here.

Figure
8.3
shows the reconstruction of the hydrodynamic generator contribution
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{G})}
(dashed blue) and the total RTA attractor
\bar{\pi}_{s}^{(\text{A})}
(dashed purple), with errors staying below
2\%
. This is accomplished by using significantly fewer perturbative corrections than what is typically needed to compute the attractor via Borel resummation
[
95
,
189
]
. Another potential advantage of this technique is that it could be applied to Bjorken expanding systems with different relaxation times. For example, it is known that a direct Borel resummation fails when the relaxation time
\tau_{r}\propto T^{-\Delta}
and
\Delta>3

[
213
]
. Additional work would be needed to verify whether or not this method is capable of constructing the attractor in these situations.

8.4
Hydrodynamic generator in (3+1)–dimensions

In (3+1)–dimensional Minkowski spacetime
x^{\mu}=(t,x,y,z)
without Bjorken symmetry, the hydrodynamic generator in the relaxation time approximation can be further generalized as a path integral along free-streaming characteristics:

f_{\text{G}}(x,p)=\int^{x}_{x_{-}}{dx^{\prime}{\cdot\,}s^{-1}(x^{\prime},p)D(x,x^{\prime},p)f_{\text{eq}}(x^{\prime},p)}\,.

(8.65)

Here the starting point
x^{\mu}_{-}=x^{\mu}-(t{-}t_{-})p^{\mu}/E
lies on a hypersurface
t_{-}=\Sigma_{-}(x,y,z)
consisting of the initial-state boundary
\Sigma_{0}
and the future light cone enclosing it (see Figure
8.4
).

Figure 8.4:

An illustration of the path integral (
8.65
) (solid gray) running from a point
(t_{-},\bm{x}_{-})
on the hypersurface
\Sigma_{-}
(solid black) to the current position
(t,\bm{x})
. The path is parallel to the particle momentum
p^{\mu}
at point
(t,\bm{x})
. (Note that
x
,
x_{-}
in the figure stand for 3-dimensional spatial vectors.)

Note that, in contrast to Eq. (
8.14
), the (3+1)–dimensional generator is not constrained by any symmetries and (after Landau matching) can accommodate any flow velocity profile
u^{\mu}(x)
, including ones with non-vanishing vorticity (which for Bjorken flow is forbidden by symmetry). The path integral (
8.65
) runs over a straight time-like characteristic line parallel to the particle momentum
p^{\mu}
, with a measure
dx^{\prime}\cdot s^{-1}(x^{\prime},p)
that (unlike the one in Eq. (
8.14
)) depends on momentum. The fraction of particles with momentum
p^{\mu}
emitted from the thermal source
f_{\text{eq}}(x^{\prime},p)
that travel freely through the medium and arrive at the current position
x^{\mu}
unscathed is given by the damping function (
8.3
). For short relaxation times this damping function decays very rapidly, limiting the range of influence on the fluid’s dynamics at position
x
to points
x^{\prime}
in the past light cone of
x
with small spacetime separations
x{-}x^{\prime}
.

Let us verify that the expansion of Eq. (
8.65
) reduces to the more general Borel resummed RTA Chapman–Enskog expansion (up to some finite order):

f^{\text{B}}_{\text{CE}}(x,p)=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\,e^{-z}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}\left[-s^{\mu}(x,p)\partial_{\mu}\right]^{n}f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)}{n!}\,.

(8.66)

Following the same steps outlined in the previous section, we use the coordinate transformation

z=h(x^{\prime},x,p)=\int^{x}_{x^{\prime}}dx^{\prime\prime}{\cdot\,}s^{-1}(x^{\prime\prime},p)

(8.67)

to rewrite Eq. (
8.65
) as

f_{\text{G}}(x,p)=\int_{0}^{z_{-}}dz\,e^{-z}f_{\text{eq}}(h^{-1}(z,x,p),p)\,,

(8.68)

where

z_{-}=\int^{x}_{x_{-}}dx^{\prime\prime}{\cdot\,}s^{-1}(x^{\prime\prime},p)\,.

(8.69)

The inverse function
h^{-1}_{\mu}(z,x,p)
is now promoted to a four-vector that can be expanded as a power series:

x^{\prime}_{\mu}=h^{-1}_{\mu}(z,x,p)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n,\mu}(x,p)\,z^{n}\,,

(8.70)

which can be inserted in the Taylor expansion of Eq. (
8.67
):

\begin{split}z=&{\int^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{0}}d\lambda^{\prime\prime}\,g(x^{\prime\prime}(\lambda^{\prime\prime}),p)={\int^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{0}}d\lambda^{\prime\prime}\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\lambda^{\prime\prime})^{n}p^{n}{\cdot}\,\partial^{n}g(x,p)}{n!}\\
=&-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\lambda^{\prime})^{n+1}p^{n}{\cdot}\,\partial^{n}g(x,p)}{(n+1)!}\,,\end{split}

(8.71)

where we used the parameterization

x^{\prime\prime\mu}(\lambda^{\prime\prime})=x^{\mu}-\lambda^{\prime\prime}p^{\mu}\,,\indent\indent(0\leq\lambda^{\prime\prime}\leq\lambda^{\prime}),

(8.72)

with
\lambda^{\prime}=(x{-}x^{\prime})\cdot p/p^{2}
and
g(x,p)=p\cdot u(x)/\tau_{r}(x)
. Using the product rule identities

\displaystyle s^{\mu}\partial_{\nu}s^{-1}_{\mu}=

\displaystyle-s^{-1}_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu}\,,

(8.73a)

\displaystyle s^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}s^{-1}_{\mu}=

\displaystyle-s^{-1}_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu}-(\partial_{\alpha}s^{-1}_{\mu})(\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu})-(\partial_{\nu}s^{-1}_{\mu})(\partial_{\alpha}s^{\mu})\,,

(8.73b)

one obtains, after some algebra, the following first coefficients of the series:

\displaystyle c_{0}^{\,\mu}

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}\,,

(8.74a)

\displaystyle c_{1}^{\,\mu}

\displaystyle=-s^{\mu}\,,

(8.74b)

\displaystyle c_{2}^{\,\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}s^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu}\,,

(8.74c)

\displaystyle c_{3}^{\,\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3!}\left((s^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}s^{\nu})\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu}+s^{\alpha}s^{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu}\right)\,,

(8.74d)

analogous to the coefficients (
8.20
). They obey the recurrence relation

\displaystyle c_{0}^{\,\mu}

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}\,,

(8.75a)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{\,\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{s^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}c_{n{-}1}^{\,\mu}}{n}\indent\forall\,n\geq 1\,,

(8.75b)

the proof of which is given in Appendix
E.1
. The integral (
8.68
) can then be Taylor expanded as

f_{\text{G}}(x,p){=}\int_{0}^{z_{-}}{dz\,e^{-z}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(h^{-1}(z,x,p)-x)^{n}\cdot f_{\text{eq}}^{(n)}(x,p)}{n!}}\,,

(8.76)

where
f_{\text{eq}}^{(n)}(x,p)=\partial^{n}f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)
. The series expansion of the hydrodynamic generator up to third order in the Knudsen number is

\begin{split}f_{\text{G}}\approx&\,(1-e^{-z_{-}})f_{\text{eq}}\,+\,\big(1-\Gamma(2,z_{-})\big)\delta f^{(1)}\,+\,\\
&\Big(1-\frac{\Gamma(3,z_{-})}{2!}\Big)\delta f^{(2)}\,+\,\Big(1-\frac{\Gamma(4,z_{-})}{3!}\Big)\delta f^{(3)}\,,\end{split}

(8.77)

which has the same structure as Eq. (
8.23
). For spacetime regions far in the future from the hypersurface
\Sigma_{-}
in Fig.
8.4
we assume we can take the limit
z_{-}\to\infty
:

f_{\text{G}}\approx f_{\text{eq}}+\delta f^{(1)}+\delta f^{(2)}+\delta f^{(3)}\,,

(8.78)

where

\displaystyle\delta f^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\,-s^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}\,,

(8.79a)

\displaystyle\delta f^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\,(s^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu})\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}\,+\,s^{\nu}s^{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}\,,

(8.79b)

\displaystyle\delta f^{(3)}=

\displaystyle\,-\left((s^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}s^{\nu})\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu}\right)\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}\,-\,(s^{\alpha}s^{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}s^{\mu})\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}

(8.79c)

\displaystyle\,-\,3s^{\mu}(s^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}s^{\nu})\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}\,-\,s^{\alpha}s^{\nu}s^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{eq}}\,.

As expected, these agree with the corresponding gradient corrections from the RTA Chapman–Enskog expansion when worked out to third order. A proof that the (3+1)–d hydrodynamic generator expansion reduces to the Borel resummation (
8.66
) at all orders in the Knudsen number is given in Appendix
E.1
.

The distribution function
f_{\text{G}}(x,p)
given in Eq. (
8.65
) is also a particular solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation in Minkowski spacetime (
8.1
):

\begin{split}s^{\mu}(x,p)\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{G}}(x,p)&=f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)-{\int^{x}_{x_{-}}}dx^{\prime}{\cdot\,}s^{-1}(x^{\prime},p)D(x,x^{\prime},p)f_{\text{eq}}(x^{\prime},p)\\
&=f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)-f_{\text{G}}(x,p)\,.\end{split}

(8.80)

Here we used the identities
s^{\mu}(x,p)\partial_{\mu}D(x,x^{\prime},p)=-D(x,x^{\prime},p)

and
D(x,x,p)=1
.
13
13

13

Note that the directional derivative
s^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
does not act on the lower limit of the path integral (
8.65
) since the current position
x^{\mu}
varies infinitesimally only along the direction of
s^{\mu}
, which means that the starting point
x^{\mu}_{-}
remains fixed.

Unlike Eq. (
8.8
), the distribution function (
8.65
) approaches zero on the entire initial-state surface
\Sigma_{0}
since it does not include any initial-state information. One can make use of the diagram in Fig.
8.4
to construct and add such an initial-state term. We know that the system is initialized at time
t_{0}
as
f_{0}(x_{0},p)
, with
x^{\mu}_{0}\in\Sigma_{0}
. In addition, we assume that the medium on
\Sigma_{0}
is surrounded by vacuum so that
f(x,p)
vanishes on its entire future light cone. Therefore, only characteristic lines that are connected to the initial-state surface
\Sigma_{0}
as shown in Fig.
8.4
will pick up an initial source that decays over time:

f_{\text{I}}(x,p)=D(x,x_{-},p)f_{0}(x_{-},p)\Theta(t_{0}-t_{-})\,.

(8.81)

Here the Heaviside step function
\Theta(t_{0}-t_{-})
excludes those characteristic lines that end on the light cones in Fig.
8.4
and thereby enforces
f_{\text{I}}(x,p)=0
if
x_{-}\notin\Sigma_{0}
. The full (3+1)–dimensional solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation (
8.1
) is then

f(x,p)=f_{\text{I}}(x,p)+f_{\text{G}}(x,p)\,.

(8.82)

One can check that

s^{\mu}(x,p)\partial_{\mu}f(x,p)=-f_{\text{I}}(x,p)+f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)-f_{\text{G}}(x,p)=f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)-f(x,p)\,,

(8.83)

where we used the relation
s^{\mu}(x,p)\partial_{\mu}D(x,x_{-},p)=-D(x,x_{-},p)
. A more formal derivation of this solution can be found in Appendix
E.4
.
14
14

14

Equation (
8.82
) generalizes the RTA Bjorken solution (
8.8
) to (3+1)–dimensional systems, by replacing the integration over the fluid’s history in
\tau^{\prime}
with one over a path parameter
\lambda^{\prime}
along a set of free-streaming past world lines. Each world line’s direction depends on the momentum of the incoming particle, emitted by either an initial source
f_{0}(x_{0},p)
or a thermal source
f_{\text{eq}}(x^{\prime},p)
. Macroscopic observables at a given spacetime coordinate
x^{\mu}
are influenced by the fluid’s history encoded in these world lines.
In the free-streaming limit
\tau_{r}\to\infty
(
z_{-}\to 0)
, the distribution function takes on the free-streaming solution
f_{0}(x_{-},p)\Theta(t_{0}{-}t_{-})
. In the ideal hydrodynamic limit
\tau_{r}\to 0
(
z_{-}\to\infty)
,
f(x,p)\to f_{\text{G}}(x,p)
, which reduces to
f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)
since
s^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f_{\text{G}}(x,p)\to 0
in Eq. (
8.80
).
15
15

15

In the ideal hydrodynamic limit, the local equilibrium density operator can only accommodate flow profiles that are irrotational
[
222
]
.

This completes our formal argument for the RTA Boltzmann equation in the (3+1)–dimensional case. We leave its numerical implementation to future work but close this section with some thoughts about how such an implementation might look. Just like the Bjorken solution (
8.8
), the distribution function (
8.82
) for (3+1)–dimensional expansion is an
implicit
solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation since it depends on the temperature
T(x)
and fluid velocity
u^{\mu}(x)
. In principle, the hydrodynamic fields can be reconstructed by matching the solution to the Landau frame:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{p}(p\cdot u(x))^{2}f(x,p)\,,

(8.84a)

\displaystyle u^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\int_{p}(p\cdot u(x))\,p^{\mu}f(x,p)}{\int_{p}(p\cdot u(x))^{2}f(x,p)}\,,

(8.84b)

where
\int_{p}=\int\dfrac{d^{3}p}{E}
. Similar to Eq. (
8.31
), these integral equations can then be solved numerically with a root-finding algorithm such as fixed-point iteration. Starting with an approximate solution for
T(x)
and
u^{\mu}(x)
, which can be provided e.g. by a viscous hydrodynamic simulation, one would repeatedly update the solution by evaluating the right-hand-side of Eq. (
8.84
). Since the initial guess and exact solution share the same initial condition but may differ greatly for later times, this numerical scheme is likely to converge faster at times near
t_{0}
than at later times. Instead of computing a single iteration across the entire evolution, as is commonly done
[
101
]
, it would here be more efficient to perform these iterations at a given time step until the solution is within the desired error tolerance, before proceeding to the next time step. Faster rates of convergence might be achievable if a more accurate hydrodynamic model is used to evolve the initial guess for the fluid’s energy density and flow profiles.

Due to the momentum dependence of the characteristic lines and their associated damping functions, solving the integral equations (
8.84
) is much more involved than for the Bjorken case. Unfortunately, there does not seem to be a way of reducing the momentum-space integral without invoking additional symmetries like in Eq. (
8.31
). This leaves us with the computationally intensive task of numerically evaluating a four-dimensional integral for each spacetime point: three for the momentum and one for the path parameter along the associated characteristic line. One possible way to reduce the computing time is to parallelize at each time step the computation over the spatial grid points. Doing this on a GPU, however, still faces memory limitations: for a uniform spacetime grid, the memory required to do a full calculation of the distribution function grows rapidly with the volume of the future light cone,
V\propto t^{4}
. For short relaxation times, rapid damping will reduce the need for RAM to only a fraction of the fluid’s evolution history. Still, the task looks formidable and will likely require a highly advanced algorithm and significant computing resources.

Based on the structure of our formal (3+1)–d solution we anticipate that effects qualitatively similar to those described in Sec.
8.2
will also be found for RTA kinetic fluids without Bjorken symmetry: at early times, the dynamics of the fluid is dominated by the non-hydrodynamic mode associated with the initial state
f_{0}(x_{0},p)
. As time moves away from the initial-state surface
\Sigma_{0}
the local-equilibrium distribution
f_{\text{eq}}(x,p)
and first-order gradient correction
\delta f^{(1)}(x,p)
quickly take over, with the higher-order corrections emerging more slowly. A quantitative analysis of the contributions from the non-equilibrium corrections
\delta f_{\text{G}}
to macroscopic observables will need to wait until the corresponding codes have been developed. Intermediate studies of systems with reduced symmetry (for example undergoing spherical expansion) may be useful for developing intuition and computational tools.

8.5
Summary

In this chapter we established a correspondence between the hydrodynamic generator and the Borel resummed Chapman–Enskog series for the (3+1)–d RTA Boltzmann equation. Instead of computing the Borel resummation directly, we used the Green’s function method to construct a map to the integral representation of the hydrodynamic gradient series in the late-time limit. Relaxing this limit, we find at earlier times a set of non-hydrodynamic modes that are coupled to the RTA Chapman–Enskog expansion. For Bjorken flow, we showed that these non-hydrodynamic modes control the emergence of hydrodynamics as an effective field theory description of non-equilibrium fluids. As the initial-state memory decays, the local-equilibrium distribution and its first-order gradient correction emerge as the leading contributors to the fluid’s dynamics. Higher-order gradient corrections to the particle distribution function are suppressed during the hydrodynamization process, especially at early times. This means that even in far-off-equilibrium situations, where the Knudsen number is initially large, these higher-order corrections are not as severe as traditionally thought.

Our formula (
8.82
) for the (3+1)–dimensional solution of the RTA Boltzmann equation in Minkowski spacetime has the nice features of being positive definite and finite for both small and large values of the Knudsen number. It is also causal since it only depends on the present and past hydrodynamic fields. While it is not immediately obvious how to numerically implement this solution, it can potentially serve as a reference to test the validity of known viscous hydrodynamic approximations, as well as the new expansion scheme described in this chapter, in the relaxation time approximation without the need for Bjorken symmetry.

Chapter 9
Conclusions and outlook

In this thesis, we have developed a set of tools to complete an event-by-event anisotropic hydrodynamic simulation of ultrarelativistic heavy-ion collisions. This was accomplished by deriving a new macroscopic set of equations for anisotropic hydrodynamics and numerically implementing them in
VAH
to model realistic nuclear collisions. We showed that our model is applicable at much earlier times when standard second-order viscous hydrodynamics breaks down. Thus, we were able to use anisotropic hydrodynamics to evolve most of the far-off-equilibrium stage
\tau\in(0,1]
fm/
c
without needing to use a separate pre-hydrodynamic module. We then merged our hydrodynamic code with the particlization module
iS3D
to study the effects our PTMA modified anisotropic distribution had on the sampled particle spectra (e.g. elliptic flow at high centralities decreased relative to the 14–moment approximation in Fig.
7.2
). In our preliminary analysis, we launched these two modules together in an end-to-end simulation to model Pb+Pb collisions at the LHC (
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=2.76
TeV) and accurately reproduced the experimental hadronic observables. This marks a significant advancement in the field of anisotropic hydrodynamics, which only recently has started being used in heavy-ion phenomenology
[
124
,
125
,
126
]
.

A more comprehensive model-to-data analysis that would involve both a re-optimization of the Bayesian model parameters and the inclusion of experimental data from other collision systems (e.g. Au+Au
\sqrt{s_{\text{NN}}}=200
GeV) will be left to future studies. Once completed, this will allow us to investigate how different selections among a discrete set of hydrodynamic models affect both the theoretical description of heavy-ion experimental observables and the shear and bulk viscosity constraints inferred from such data–theory comparisons. In particular, it would be interesting to test the predictions from anisotropic hydrodynamics against a variety of initializations of second-order viscous hydrodynamics, using different pre-hydrodynamic evolution models (or no pre-hydrodynamic stage at all), similar in spirit to the recent study done in Ref.
[
106
]
.

The current version of our code only evolves the quark-gluon plasma’s energy-momentum tensor components and ignores the effects of net baryon density and diffusion. Second-order viscous hydrodynamic codes with nonzero
n_{B}
and
V_{B}^{\mu}
have already been practically implemented
[
223
,
183
]
, and others that initialize viscous hydrodynamics with dynamical sources
[
27
,
44
,
45
,
47
,
48
]
are currently under development. The
BEShydro
code
[
47
,
48
]
, in particular, shares a common ancestry
[
103
]
and therefore a number of similar features with
VAH
. However, we have not yet considered the effects of nonzero chemical potentials for net baryon number, electric charge and strangeness in our formulation of anisotropic hydrodynamics. An upgrade of our code package that includes them is left to future work.

We note that anisotropic hydrodynamics is a sub-field of far-from-equilibrium hydrodynamics, which has only been around for about a decade. There are still many things left to learn about the remarkable applicability of hydrodynamics in systems with large gradients. In Chapter
8
, we introduced a new technique based on Green’s functions to extract the resummed gradient series of the linear RTA Boltzmann equation. It would be much more difficult to derive a hydrodynamic generator for the full nonlinear Boltzmann equation, which generally has a much more complex collision kernel. For strongly coupled fluids whose multi-particle correlations cannot be neglected, we would also have to consider stochastic fluctuations and the microscopic correlations that they generate
[
224
,
41
,
225
]
. However, if we perturb the system around local equilibrium (or generally, around some known background
[
226
,
227
]
), it might be possible to at least derive an approximation for the corresponding Green’s function from either the Boltzmann equation or a Kadanoff–Baym type equation
[
228
]
. It remains an open question whether or not the concept of a hydrodynamic generator can be extended to other systems, but if it turns out that constructing them is possible, we could potentially use them to develop better hydrodynamic models for far-from-equilibrium fluids.

Appendix A

A.1
Anisotropic integrals in conformal Gubser flow

Here we calculate the conformal anisotropic thermal integrals
\hat{I}_{nlq}
and
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{nlr}
that appear Chapter
2
. First we define

\hat{I}_{nlq}\big(\hat{\Lambda},\xi\big)=\int_{\hat{p}}(\hat{p}^{\rho})^{n-l-2q}\,\hat{p}_{\eta}^{\,l}\bigg(\frac{\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2}}{\cosh^{2}\!\rho}\bigg)^{q}f_{a}\,,

(A.1)

where the anisotropic distribution
f_{a}
is given by Eq. (
2.21
). The change of variables

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{p}_{\theta}}{\cosh\rho}

\displaystyle=\lambda\,\sin\alpha\,\cos\beta\,,

(A.2a)

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{p}_{\phi}}{\cosh\rho\,\sin\theta}

\displaystyle=\lambda\,\sin\alpha\,\sin\beta\,,

(A.2b)

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{\eta}

\displaystyle=\lambda\,(1{+}\xi)^{-1/2}\cos\alpha

(A.2c)

leads to the following factorization of the integral (
A.1
):

\hat{I}_{nlq}\big(\hat{\Lambda},\xi\big)=\,\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\big(\hat{\Lambda}\big)\,\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{nlq}\left(\xi\right)\,,

(A.3)

where

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n}\big(\hat{\Lambda}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\lambda}{2\pi^{2}}\,\lambda^{n+1}\,e^{-\lambda/\hat{\Lambda}}=\frac{(n{+}1)!}{2\pi^{2}}\,\hat{\Lambda}^{n+2},

(A.4)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{nlq}\left(\xi\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2(1{+}\xi)^{\left(n-2q\right)/2}}\int_{-1}^{1}d\cos\alpha\left(\sin\alpha\right)^{2q}\left(\cos\alpha\right)^{l}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\times\left[(1{+}\xi)\sin^{2}\!\alpha+\cos^{2}\!\alpha\right]^{(n-l-2q-1)/2}\,.

The
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{nlq}
functions that appear in Chapter
2
are

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{200}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{1{+}\xi}+t(\xi)\right)

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{200}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\xi}\left(\frac{1{-}\xi}{(1{+}\xi)^{2}}-t(\xi)\right)

(A.6a)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{201}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\xi}\left(1+(\xi{-}1)t(\xi)\right)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{220}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\xi}\left(-\frac{1}{1{+}\xi}+t(\xi)\right)

(A.6b)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{240}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\xi^{2}}\left(\frac{3{+}2\xi}{1{+}\xi}-3t(\xi)\right)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{301}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3(1{+}\xi)^{3/2}}

(A.6c)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{320}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3(1{+}\xi)^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{340}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}(1{+}\xi)^{1/2}}\left(-\frac{(3{+}4\xi)}{3(1{+}\xi)}+h(\xi)\right)

(A.6d)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{400}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{5{+}3\xi}{(1{+}\xi)^{2}}+3t(\xi)\right)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{420}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\xi}\left(\frac{\xi{-}1}{(1{+}\xi)^{2}}+t(\xi)\right)

(A.6e)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{440}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\xi^{2}}\left(-\frac{3{+}5\xi}{(1{+}\xi)^{2}}+3t(\xi)\right)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{460}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\xi^{3}}\left(\frac{8\xi^{2}{+}25\xi{+}15}{(1{+}\xi)^{2}}-15t(\xi)\right)\,,

(A.6f)

where
t(\xi)=\arctan\sqrt{\xi}/\sqrt{\xi}
and
h(\xi)=\text{arctanh}\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{1{+}\xi}}/\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{1{+}\xi}}
. In Section
2.2
we needed the moments
\hat{I}_{nlq}^{\mathrm{eq}}
associated with the equilibrium distribution function. They are obtained as the isotropic limit of Eq. (
A.3
):

\hat{I}_{nlq}^{\mathrm{eq}}(\hat{T})\equiv\lim_{\xi\to 0}\hat{I}_{nlq}\big(\hat{\Lambda},\xi\big)=\hat{I}_{nlq}\big(\hat{T},0)\,.

(A.7)

Finally, we define the anisotropic integrals

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{nlr}\big(\hat{\Lambda},\xi\big)=\int_{\hat{p}}\!\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}^{\,n{-}l}\,\hat{p}_{\eta}^{\,l}\,E_{\mathrm{RS}}^{r}\,f_{a}\,.

(A.8)

With the change of variables (
A.2
) one can show that

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{nlr}\big(\hat{\Lambda},\xi\big)=\hat{\mathcal{J}}_{n+r}\big(\hat{\Lambda}\big)\,\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{nl0}\left(\xi\right).

(A.9)

A.2
14–moment approximation in the NLO NRS scheme

In this section we present the calculation of the four coefficients which enter into the 14–moment approximation (
2.52
) for
\delta\tilde{f}
. We begin with the most general 14–moment ansatz for anisotropic fluids

[
113
]
and decompose it in LRF coordinates for Gubser flow, using the notation
\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}\equiv\hat{p}^{\rho}=-\hat{u}\cdot\hat{p}
:

\begin{split}\delta\tilde{f}\approx\delta\tilde{f}_{14}&=\left[\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}_{\mu}\hat{p}^{\mu}+\hat{\omega}_{\mu\nu}\hat{p}^{\mu}\hat{p}^{\nu}\right]f_{a}\\
&=\left[\hat{\alpha}-\hat{\beta}\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}+\hat{\omega}\Big(\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}^{2}{+}\textstyle{\frac{1}{3}}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}\Big)+\Big(\hat{\beta}_{\langle\mu\rangle}{-}2\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}\,\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\rangle}\Big)\hat{p}^{\langle\mu\rangle}+\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}\hat{p}^{\langle\mu}\hat{p}^{\nu\rangle}\right]f_{a}\,.\end{split}

(A.10)

Here we introduced
\hat{\beta}\equiv-\hat{\beta}_{\mu}\hat{u}^{\mu}
,
\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}\equiv\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2}/\cosh^{2}\rho+\hat{p}_{\eta}^{2}
,
\hat{\omega}\equiv\hat{\omega}^{\mu\nu}\hat{u}_{\mu}\hat{u}_{\nu}
,
\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\rangle}=-\hat{\Delta}_{\mu\nu}\hat{\omega}^{\nu\lambda}\hat{u}_{\lambda}

\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}=\hat{\Delta}^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}\hat{\omega}_{\alpha\beta}
. This expression can be simplified by using the constraints imposed by Gubser symmetry and Landau matching. In the Landau frame and in the absence of chemical potentials, the spatial vectors
\hat{\beta}_{\langle\mu\rangle}
and
\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\rangle}
vanish, respectively. Due to the
\text{SO}(3)_{q}
symmetry, the traceless symmetric spatial tensor
\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}
has only one independent component, namely
\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}
:

\hat{\omega}_{\langle\mu\nu\rangle}=\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}\,\text{diag}\left(0,-\cosh^{2}\rho,-\cosh^{2}\rho\sin^{2}\theta,1\right)\,.

(A.11)

Using the mass-shell condition to eliminate
\hat{p}_{\Omega}^{2}
, Eq. (
A.10
) thus reduces to

\delta\tilde{f}_{14}=\left[\hat{\alpha}-\hat{\beta}\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}+\hat{\omega}\left(\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}^{2}{+}\textstyle{\frac{1}{3}}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}\right)+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}\left(3\hat{p}_{\eta}^{2}{-}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}\right)\right]f_{a}\,.

(A.12)

The four coefficients
\hat{\alpha}
,
\hat{\beta}
,
\hat{\omega}
, and
\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}
must be matched to the contributions of the residual deviation
\delta\tilde{f}
to the particle and energy densities as well as the bulk and shear viscous pressures
[
113
]
:

\displaystyle\delta\hat{\tilde{n}}

\displaystyle\equiv-\langle(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{p})\rangle_{\tilde{\delta}}=0\,,

(A.13a)

\displaystyle\delta\hat{\tilde{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{p})^{2}\rangle_{\tilde{\delta}}=0\,,

(A.13b)

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\Pi}}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{3}\langle\hat{\Delta}_{\mu\nu}\hat{p}^{\mu}\hat{p}^{\nu}\rangle_{\tilde{\delta}}=\frac{1}{3}\langle\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}\rangle_{\tilde{\delta}}\,,

(A.13c)

\displaystyle\hat{\tilde{\pi}}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle\hat{p}^{\langle\eta}\hat{p}^{\eta\rangle}\rangle_{\tilde{\delta}}=\langle\hat{p}^{2}_{\eta}-\frac{1}{3}\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}\rangle_{\tilde{\delta}}\,.

(A.13d)

For conformal systems the bulk viscous pressure vanishes; for technical reasons we introduce an infinitesimal fictitious mass
m
for the particles, approaching the conformal limit at the end by setting
m\to 0
. This is the reason why in Eq. (
A.12
) we did not replace
\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}
by
\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}^{2}
.

Inserting the ansatz (
A.12
) into Eq. (
A.13
) and using
\hat{\mathbf{p}}^{2}=\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}^{2}{-}m^{2}
one obtains the matrix equation
\mathcal{A}\,b=c
, where

b=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c c c}\hat{\alpha}&\hat{\beta}&\hat{\omega}&\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}\end{array}\right)^{T},\qquad c=\big(\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&0&3\hat{\tilde{\Pi}}&\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\end{array}\big)^{T},

(A.14)

\mathcal{A}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}\hat{I}_{100}&-\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{300}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{320}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{300}\\
\\
\hat{I}_{200}&-\hat{I}_{300}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{400}{-}\frac{m^{2}}{3}\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{420}{-}\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{400}{+}\frac{m^{2}}{2}\hat{I}_{200}\\
\\
\hat{I}_{200}{-}m^{2}\hat{I}_{000}&m^{2}\hat{I}_{100}{-}\hat{I}_{300}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{400}{-}\frac{5m^{2}}{3}\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{420}{-}\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{400}{+}m^{2}(\hat{I}_{200}{-}\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{220})\\
\\
\hat{I}_{220}{-}\frac{1}{3}\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{1}{3}\hat{I}_{300}{-}\hat{I}_{320}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{420}{-}\frac{4}{9}\hat{I}_{400}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{440}{-}\hat{I}_{420}{+}\frac{1}{6}\hat{I}_{400}\\
\end{array}\right]

(A.15)

Here we expanded the
2^{\text{nd}}
and
3^{\text{rd}}
rows in
m
, keeping only terms up to
\mathcal{O}(m^{2})
. After subtracting the
2^{\text{nd}}
from the
3^{\text{rd}}
row and rescaling it by
-1/m^{2}
, the matrix equation becomes
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\,b=c^{\prime}
, where

c^{\prime}=\big(\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&0&\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{0}&\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\end{array}\big)^{T},

(A.16)

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}\hat{I}_{100}&-\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{300}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{320}{-}\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{300}\\
\\
\hat{I}_{200}&-\hat{I}_{300}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{400}{-}\frac{m^{2}}{3}\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{420}{-}\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{400}{+}\frac{m^{2}}{2}\hat{I}_{200}\\
\\
\hat{I}_{000}&-\hat{I}_{100}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{220}{-}\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{200}\\
\\
\hat{I}_{220}{-}\frac{1}{3}\hat{I}_{200}&\frac{1}{3}\hat{I}_{300}{-}\hat{I}_{320}&\frac{4}{3}\hat{I}_{420}{-}\frac{4}{9}\hat{I}_{400}&\frac{3}{2}\hat{I}_{440}{-}\hat{I}_{420}{+}\frac{1}{6}\hat{I}_{400}\\
\end{array}\right]\,,

(A.17)

with
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{0}\equiv-3\hat{\tilde{\Pi}}/m^{2}
being the irreducible
\delta\tilde{f}
-moment associated with the residual bulk viscous pressure. In the conformal limit we set in Eq. (
A.17
) the mass
m
and the scalar moment
\hat{\tilde{\rho}}_{0}
to zero. The matrix equation can now be easily inverted. One finds

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\alpha_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{2}(\hat{\Lambda})}\,,\qquad\hat{\beta}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\beta_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{3}(\hat{\Lambda})}\,,\qquad\hat{\omega}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\gamma_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{4}(\hat{\Lambda})}\,,\qquad\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}=\frac{\hat{\tilde{\pi}}\,\kappa_{\tilde{\pi}}(\xi)}{\mathcal{J}_{4}(\hat{\Lambda})},

(A.18)

where the functions
\alpha_{\tilde{\pi}}
,
\beta_{\tilde{\pi}}
,
\gamma_{\tilde{\pi}}
and
\kappa_{\tilde{\pi}}
are given by

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\alpha_{\tilde{\pi}}=\frac{-3\xi\left(2(39{+}76\xi){-}(213+343\xi+104\xi^{2})t(\xi){+}90(1{+}\xi)t^{2}(\xi){+}45(1{+}\xi)^{3}t^{3}(\xi)\right)}{192+6(131{+}212\xi)t(\xi){-}(1932{+}2817\xi{+}712\xi^{2})t^{2}(\xi){+}630(1{+}\xi)t^{3}(\xi){+}315(1{+}\xi)^{3}t^{4}(\xi)},\end{gathered}

(A.19)

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\beta_{\tilde{\pi}}=\frac{24\xi\sqrt{1{+}\xi}\left(12{+}(15{+}13\xi)t(\xi){-}(27{+}26\xi{-}\xi^{2})t^{2}(\xi)\right)}{192+6(131{+}212\xi)t(\xi){-}(1932{+}2817\xi{+}712\xi^{2})t^{2}(\xi){+}630(1{+}\xi)t^{3}(\xi){+}315(1{+}\xi)^{3}t^{4}(\xi)},\end{gathered}

(A.20)

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\gamma_{\tilde{\pi}}=\frac{5\xi}{2}\frac{3(57{+}95\xi{+}4\xi^{2}){-}2(99{+}234\xi{+}231\xi^{2}{+}122\xi^{3})t(\xi){+}9(9{+}19\xi{+}31\xi^{2}{+}21\xi^{3})t^{2}(\xi){+}54(1{+}\xi)^{3}(2\xi{-}1)t^{3}(\xi)}{192{+}6(212\xi{+}131)t(\xi){-}(1923{+}2817\xi{+}712\xi^{2})t^{2}(\xi){+}630(1{+}\xi)t^{3}(\xi){+}315(1{+}\xi)^{3}t^{4}(\xi)},\end{gathered}

(A.21)

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\kappa_{\tilde{\pi}}=\frac{20\xi^{2}}{3}\frac{3(4\xi{-}13){-}(162{+}297\xi{+}122\xi^{2})t(\xi){+}9(31{+}52\xi{+}21\xi^{2})t^{2}(\xi){+}108(1{+}\xi)^{3}t^{3}(\xi)}{192{+}6(212\xi{+}131)t(\xi){-}(1923{+}2817\xi{+}712\xi^{2})t^{2}(\xi){+}630(1{+}\xi)t^{3}(\xi){+}315(1{+}\xi)^{3}t^{4}(\xi)}.\end{gathered}

(A.22)

In the massless limit Eq. (
A.12
) coincides with Eq. (
2.52
) in the text. As a cross-check we consider the isotropic limit
\xi\rightarrow 0
of the coefficients (
A.18
):

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}\to 0,\qquad\hat{\beta}\to 0,\qquad\hat{\omega}\to 0,\qquad\hat{\omega}_{\langle\eta\eta\rangle}\to\frac{\hat{\pi}}{2(\hat{\mathcal{E}}{+}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{eq}})\hat{T}^{2}}.

(A.23)

With this we see that Eq. (
2.52
) reduces in the isotropic limit to the well-known Israel-Stewart result
[
166
]
for conformal systems
[
229
]
:

\delta\tilde{f}_{14}\to\left[\frac{\hat{\pi}}{4(\hat{\mathcal{E}}{+}\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{eq}})\hat{T}^{2}}\left(3p_{\eta}^{2}-\hat{E}_{\hat{p}}^{2}\right)\right]f_{\text{eq}}.

(A.24)

Appendix B

B.1
Nonconformal anisotropic integrals

In this section, we define the nonconformal anisotropic integrals that appear in Chapters
3
–
5
and show how to integrate them numerically. The anisotropic integrals
\mathcal{I}_{nrqs}
and
\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}
are defined as

\mathcal{I}_{nrqs}=\int_{p}\frac{(u\cdot p)^{n-r-2q}}{(2q)!!}(-z\cdot p)^{r}(-p\cdot\Xi\cdot p)^{q}(p\cdot\Omega\cdot p)^{s/2}f_{a}\,,

(B.1)

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\int_{p}\frac{(u\cdot p)^{n-r-2q}}{(2q)!!}(-z\cdot p)^{r}(-p\cdot\Xi\cdot p)^{q}(p\cdot\Omega\cdot p)^{s/2}f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}\,,

(B.2)

where
f_{a}
is given by Eq. (
3.13
). For particles with Boltzmann statistics
\mathcal{I}_{nrqs}=\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}
. Although there is no known analytical solution for these integrals for massive particles, their dimensionality can be reduced to one. After substituting the spherical coordinates

\displaystyle p_{x,\mathrm{LRF}}=\alpha_{\perp}\Lambda\,\bar{p}\sin\theta\cos\phi\,,

(B.3)

\displaystyle p_{y,\mathrm{LRF}}=\alpha_{\perp}\Lambda\,\bar{p}\sin\theta\sin\phi\,,

\displaystyle p_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}=\alpha_{L}\Lambda\,\bar{p}\cos\theta\,,

with
\bar{p}=p/\Lambda
, the angular integrals in Eqs. (
B.1
) – (
B.2
) can be evaluated analytically, yielding

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{nrqs}=\,&\frac{\alpha_{\perp}^{2q+2}\alpha_{L}^{r+1}\Lambda^{n+s+2}}{4\pi^{2}(2q)!!}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{p}\,\bar{p}^{n+1}\,(\bar{p}^{2}{+}\bar{m}^{2})^{s/2}\mathcal{R}_{nrq}(\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L};\bar{m}/\bar{p})\,f_{\mathrm{eq}}(\bar{p},\bar{m})\,,\end{split}

(B.4)

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\,&\frac{\alpha_{\perp}^{2q+2}\alpha_{L}^{r+1}\Lambda^{n+s+2}}{4\pi^{2}(2q)!!}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\bar{p}\,\bar{p}^{n+1}\,(\bar{p}^{2}{+}\bar{m}^{2})^{s/2}\mathcal{R}_{nrq}(\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L};\bar{m}/\bar{p})\,f_{\mathrm{eq}}(\bar{p},\bar{m})\bar{f}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\bar{p},\bar{m})\,,\end{split}

(B.5)

where
\bar{m}=m/\Lambda
,
f_{\text{eq}}(\bar{p},\bar{m})=\bigg(\exp\left[\sqrt{\bar{p}^{2}+\bar{m}^{2}}\right]+\Theta\bigg)^{-1}
,
\bar{f}_{\mathrm{eq}}(\bar{p},\bar{m})=1-\Theta f_{\text{eq}}(\bar{p},\bar{m})
and the functions
\mathcal{R}_{nrq}
are defined as

\mathcal{R}_{nrq}(\alpha_{\perp},\alpha_{L};\bar{m}/\bar{p})=w^{\,n-r-2q-1}\int_{-1}^{1}{d}{\cos\theta}(\cos\theta)^{r}(\sin\theta)^{2q}(1{+}z\sin^{2}\theta)^{(n-r-2q-1)/2}\,,

(B.6)

with
w=\sqrt{\alpha_{L}^{2}+(\bar{m}/\bar{p})^{2}}
and
z=(\alpha_{\perp}^{2}-\alpha_{L}^{2})/w^{2}
. The radial momentum integral can be computed numerically with generalized Gauss-Laguerre quadrature. For reference, we list the functions
\mathcal{R}_{nrq}
that appear in the anisotropic hydrodynamic equations:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{200}

\displaystyle=w\big(1+(1{+}z)t(z)\big)\,,

(B.7a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{220}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1+(1{+}z)t(z)}{zw}\,,

(B.7b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{201}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+(z{-}1)t(z)}{zw}\,,

(B.7c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{240}

\displaystyle=\frac{3+2z-3(1{+}z)t(z)}{z^{2}w^{3}}\,,

(B.7d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{202}

\displaystyle=\frac{3+z+(1{+}z)(z{-}3)t(z)}{z^{2}(1{+}z)w^{3}}\,,

(B.7e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{221}

\displaystyle=\frac{-3+(3{+}z)t(z)}{z^{2}w^{3}}\,,

(B.7f)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{441}

\displaystyle=\frac{-15+13z+3(1{+}z)(5{+}z)t(z)}{4z^{3}w^{3}}\,,

(B.7g)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{402}

\displaystyle=\frac{3(z{-}1)+(z(3z{-}2)+3)t(z)}{4z^{2}w}\,,

(B.7h)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{421}

\displaystyle=\frac{3+z+(1{+}z)(z{-}3)t(z)}{4z^{2}w}\,,

(B.7i)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{422}

\displaystyle=\frac{15+z+(z(z{-}6)-15)t(z)}{4z^{3}w^{3}}\,,

(B.7j)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{403}

\displaystyle=\frac{(z{-}3)(5{+}3z)+3(1{+}z)(z(z{-}2)+5)t(z)}{4z^{3}(1{+}z)w^{3}}\,,

(B.7k)

where
t(z)=\arctan\!{\sqrt{z}}/\sqrt{z}
.

B.2
Anisotropic integral identities

Here we show how to derive the identities (
4.66
). First, we express the anisotropic integrals (
B.1
) – (
B.2
) as

\mathcal{I}_{nrqs}=\frac{1}{(2q)!!}\int_{p}E_{\mathrm{LRF}}^{n-r-2q}\,p_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}^{r}\,p_{\perp,\mathrm{LRF}}^{2q}\,E_{a}^{s}\,f_{a}\,,

(B.8)

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\frac{1}{(2q)!!}\int_{p}E_{\mathrm{LRF}}^{n-r-2q}\,p_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}^{r}\,p_{\perp,\mathrm{LRF}}^{2q}\,E_{a}^{s}\,f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}\,,

(B.9)

where

E_{a}=\sqrt{m^{2}+\dfrac{p_{\perp,\mathrm{LRF}}^{2}}{\alpha_{\perp}^{2}}+\dfrac{p_{z,\mathrm{LRF}}^{2}}{\alpha_{L}^{2}}}\,.

(B.10)

From here on out, we will suppress the LRF subscripts. To obtain the first identity (
4.66
a), we introduce rapidity coordinates

\displaystyle E=m_{\perp}\cosh{y}\,,\qquad p_{z}=m_{\perp}\sinh{y}\,,

(B.11)

where
m_{\perp}=\sqrt{m^{2}+p_{\perp}^{2}}
, to rewrite Eq. (
B.9
) as

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=g\int\frac{dy\,d^{2}p_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{3}(2q)!!}\,m_{\perp}^{n-2q}(\cosh{y})^{n-r-2q}\,(\sinh{y})^{r}\,p_{\perp}^{2q}\,E_{a}^{s}\,f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}\,.

(B.12)

Next, one can write the distribution term
f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}
as

f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}=-\frac{\Lambda\,\alpha_{L}^{2}\,E_{a}}{m_{\perp}^{2}\cosh{y}\,\sinh{y}}\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial y}\,.

(B.13)

After integrating by parts with respect to the variable
y
(where the boundary term vanishes for
r\geq 2
) one obtains the relation

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\Lambda\alpha_{L}^{2}(n{-}r{-}2q{-}1)\mathcal{I}_{n-2,r,q,s+1}+\Lambda\alpha_{L}^{2}(r{-}1)\mathcal{I}_{n-2,r-2,q,s+1}+\Lambda(s{+}1)\mathcal{I}_{n,r,q,s-1}\,,

(B.14)

which for
(n,r,q,s)=(4,2,0,-1)
yields Eq. (
4.66
a).

For the second identity (
4.66
b) one uses cylindrical coordinates

\displaystyle E=\sqrt{m^{2}+p_{\perp}^{2}+p_{z}^{2}}\,,\qquad p_{x}=p_{\perp}\cos\phi\,,\qquad p_{y}=p_{\perp}\sin\phi

(B.15)

to express the integral (
B.9
) as

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\,&g\int\frac{dp_{\perp}dp_{z}}{(2\pi)^{2}(2q)!!}\,E^{n-r-2q-1}\,p_{z}^{r}\,p_{\perp}^{2q+1}\,E_{a}^{s}\,f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}\,.\end{split}

The term
f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}
can be written as

f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}=-\frac{\Lambda\,\alpha_{\perp}^{2}\,E_{a}}{p_{\perp}}\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial p_{\perp}}\,.

(B.16)

Integration by parts with respect to
p_{\perp}
, with the boundary term vanishing for
q\geq 1
, gives

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\Lambda\alpha_{\perp}^{2}(n{-}r{-}2q{-}1)\mathcal{I}_{n-2,r,q,s+1}+\Lambda\alpha_{\perp}^{2}\mathcal{I}_{n-2,r,q-1,s+1}+\Lambda(s{+}1)\mathcal{I}_{n,r,q,s-1}\,,

(B.17)

which for
(n,r,q,s)=(4,0,1,-1)
yields Eq. (
4.66
b).

Finally, to get the third identity (
4.66
c) we use spherical coordinates

\displaystyle E=\sqrt{m^{2}+p^{2}}\,,\qquad p_{z}=p\cos\theta\,,\qquad p_{x}=p\sin\theta\cos\phi\,,\qquad p_{y}=p\sin\theta\sin\phi

(B.18)

in the integral (
B.9
)

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=g\int\frac{dp\,d\cos\theta}{(2\pi)^{2}(2q)!!}\,E^{n-r-2q-1}\,p^{r+2q+2}\,(\cos\theta)^{r}\,(\sin\theta)^{2q}\,E_{a}^{s}\,f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}

(B.19)

and write the term
f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}
as

f_{a}\bar{f}_{a}=-\frac{\Lambda\,\alpha_{\perp}^{2}\alpha_{L}^{2}}{\alpha_{\perp}^{2}{-}\alpha_{L}^{2}}\,\frac{E_{a}}{p^{2}\cos\theta}\,\frac{\partial f_{a}}{\partial\cos\theta}\,.

(B.20)

Integrating by parts with respect to
\cos\theta
, where the boundary term vanishes for
q\geq 1
, gives the following relation:

\mathcal{J}_{nrqs}=\frac{\Lambda\,\alpha_{\perp}^{2}\alpha_{L}^{2}}{\alpha_{\perp}^{2}{-}\alpha_{L}^{2}}\Bigl((r{-}1)\,\mathcal{I}_{n-2,r-2,q,s+1}{-}\mathcal{I}_{n-2,r,q-1,s+1}\Bigr)+\Lambda\,(s{+}1)\,\mathcal{I}_{n,r,q,s-1}\,,

(B.21)

which for
(n,r,q,s)=(4,2,1,-1)
yields Eq. (
4.66
c).

B.3
Anisotropic transport coefficients (kinetic part)

Here we list the kinetic contribution to the transport coefficients (labeled as
(k)
) appearing in the relaxation equations (
3.30
a-d) for the kinetic longitudinal pressure
\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}^{L(k)}_{z}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{2400}-3\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L})\mathcal{I}_{0200}\,,

(B.22a)

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}^{L(k)}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{2210}-\mathcal{P}_{L}^{(k)}+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\perp})\mathcal{I}_{0200}\,,

(B.22b)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{Wu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{J}_{4410}}{\mathcal{J}_{4210}}+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{I}_{0200}\,,

(B.22c)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{W\perp}

\displaystyle=1-\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{Wu}\,,

(B.22d)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{L(k)}_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{J}_{4220}}{\mathcal{J}_{4020}}+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{I}_{0200}

(B.22e)

and kinetic transverse pressure
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}^{\perp(k)}_{z}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{2210}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{L})\mathcal{I}_{0010}\,,

(B.23a)

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}^{\perp(k)}_{\perp}

\displaystyle=2\bigl(\mathcal{I}_{2020}-\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{(k)}\bigr)+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\perp})\mathcal{I}_{0010}\,,

(B.23b)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{W\perp}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\,\mathcal{J}_{4220}}{\mathcal{J}_{4210}}+m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{I}_{0010}\,,

(B.23c)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{Wu}

\displaystyle=\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{W\perp}-1\,,

(B.23d)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{\perp(k)}_{\pi}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{3\,\mathcal{J}_{4030}}{\mathcal{J}_{4020}}-m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{I}_{0010}\,.

(B.23e)

B.4
Second-order viscous hydrodynamics

Here we derive the second-order viscous hydrodynamic equations and their transport coefficients. We start with the quasiparticle case (
VH
) and derive the relaxation equation for
\delta B
and its second-order solution (
3.60
). The general evolution equation for
\delta B
is given by
[
170
]

\delta\dot{B}=-\frac{\delta B}{\tau_{\Pi}}+\frac{\dot{m}}{m}\left(3\Pi+4\,\delta B\right)\,,

(B.24)

where the
\dot{m}/m
term can be written as

\frac{\dot{m}}{m}=\frac{1}{m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\dot{\mathcal{E}}\,.

(B.25)

We replace the time derivative
\dot{\mathcal{E}}
with the energy conservation law in viscous hydrodynamics

\dot{\mathcal{E}}+(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}{+}\Pi)\theta-\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}=0\,,

(B.26)

where
\theta=\partial_{\mu}u^{\mu}
is the scalar expansion rate and
\sigma_{\mu\nu}=\Delta^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\beta}u_{\alpha}
is the velocity shear tensor, where
\Delta^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\Delta^{\beta}_{\nu}+\Delta^{\beta}_{\mu}\Delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}-\frac{2}{3}\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\Delta_{\mu\nu})
is the traceless double spatial projector and
\Delta^{{\mu\nu}}=g^{\mu\nu}-u^{\mu}u^{\nu}
the spatial projector. For the second-order relaxation equation, we only need the first-order approximation
\dot{\mathcal{E}}\approx-(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})\theta
, thus

\frac{\dot{m}}{m}\approx-\frac{1}{m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})\theta\,.

(B.27)

The equation of motion for
\delta B
then reduces to

\delta\dot{B}=-\frac{\delta B}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}}{m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\left(3\Pi{+}4\delta B\right)\theta\,.

(B.28)

To first order in deviations from equilibrium
\delta B
= 0. The second-order solution is given by Eq. (
3.60
)
[
170
]
. In Eq. (
B.28
), we truncate the third-order term
\propto\delta B\,\theta
to arrive at the second-order relaxation equation

\delta\dot{B}=-\frac{\delta B}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\frac{3(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})}{m}\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}\Pi\,\theta\,.

(B.29)

Next, we derive the relaxation equations for the viscous components
\Pi
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}
in the same manner as in Sec.
3.2
. We start by taking the co-moving time derivative of their quasi-kinetic definitions
[
170
]

\displaystyle\dot{\Pi}=\frac{1}{3}\,D\int_{p}(-p\cdot\Delta\cdot p)\delta f-\delta\dot{B}\,,

(B.30a)

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}^{\langle{\mu\nu}\rangle}=\frac{1}{3}\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\beta}\,D\int_{p}p^{\langle\alpha}\,p^{\beta\rangle}\delta f\,,

(B.30b)

where
\delta f
is the non-equilibrium correction to the distribution function

f=f_{\mathrm{eq}}+\delta f\,,

(B.31)

with
f_{\mathrm{eq}}=\exp\big({-}u{\,\cdot\,}p/T\big)
being the local-equilibrium Boltzmann distribution. The time derivative
\delta\dot{B}
is given by Eq. (
B.29
). We substitute the terms containing
\delta\dot{f}
using the Boltzmann equation (
3.11
) and Eq. (
B.31
):

\delta\dot{f}=-\dot{f}_{\mathrm{eq}}+\frac{C[f]-m\,\partial^{\mu}m\,\partial_{\mu}^{(p)}f}{u\cdot p}-\frac{p^{\langle\mu\rangle}\nabla_{\mu}f_{\mathrm{eq}}}{u\cdot p}-\frac{p^{\langle\mu\rangle}\nabla_{\mu}\delta f}{u\cdot p}\,,

(B.32)

where
p^{\langle\mu\rangle}=\Delta^{\mu}_{\nu}p^{\nu}
and
\nabla_{\mu}=\Delta^{\nu}_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}
is the spatial gradient. To close the system of equations we use the 14–moment approximation for
\delta f

[
70
]
:

\frac{\delta f}{f_{\mathrm{eq}}}=c_{\mathcal{E}}(u\cdot p)^{2}+\frac{1}{3}c_{\Pi}\,(-p\cdot\Delta\cdot p)+c_{\pi}^{\langle{\mu\nu}\rangle}p_{\langle\mu}\,p_{\nu\rangle}\,,

(B.33)

where
p_{\langle\mu}\,p_{\nu\rangle}
=
\Delta^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}
. To solve for the coefficients we insert the expression (
B.33
) into the energy matching condition and the definitions of
\Pi
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}
:

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=\int_{p}(u\cdot p)^{2}\delta f=0\,,

(B.34a)

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\int_{p}(-p\cdot\Delta\cdot p)\,\delta f\,,

(B.34b)

\displaystyle\pi^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\int_{p}p^{\langle\mu}\,p^{\nu\rangle}\delta f\,.

(B.34c)

Since the 14–moment approximation is first-order in the dissipative flows, we neglect the second-order contribution
{\sim\,}\delta B
to the energy density and bulk viscous pressure in Eq. (
B.34
). After some algebra, the coefficients are

\displaystyle c_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathcal{I}_{41}\,\Pi}{\frac{5}{3}\mathcal{I}_{40}I_{42}-\mathcal{I}_{41}^{2}}\,,

(B.35a)

\displaystyle c_{\Pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{I}_{40}\,\Pi}{\frac{5}{3}\mathcal{I}_{40}I_{42}-\mathcal{I}_{41}^{2}}\,,

(B.35b)

\displaystyle c_{\pi}^{\langle{\mu\nu}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{\mu\nu}}{2\,\mathcal{I}_{42}}\,,

(B.35c)

where we defined the thermodynamic integrals

\mathcal{I}_{nq}=\int_{p}\frac{(u\cdot p)^{n-2q}}{(2q{+}1)!!}(-p\cdot\Delta\cdot p)^{q}f_{\mathrm{eq}}\,.

(B.36)

The final expression for
\delta f
is

\frac{\delta f}{f_{\mathrm{eq}}}=\Big(\bar{c}_{\mathcal{E}}(u\cdot p)^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\bar{c}_{\Pi}\,(-p\cdot\Delta\cdot p)\Big)\Pi\,+\,\frac{1}{2}\bar{c}_{\pi}p_{\langle\mu}\,p_{\nu\rangle}\pi^{\mu\nu}\,,

(B.37)

where
\bar{c}_{\mathcal{E}}=c_{\mathcal{E}}/\Pi
,
\bar{c}_{\Pi}=c_{\Pi}/\Pi
and
\bar{c}_{\pi}=1/\mathcal{I}_{42}
. After integration by parts and inserting for
\delta f
the 14–moment approximation (
B.37
), the relaxation equations for
\Pi
and
\pi^{\mu\nu}
reduce to

\displaystyle\dot{\Pi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Pi}{\tau_{\Pi}}-\beta_{\Pi}\theta-\delta_{\Pi\Pi}\Pi\theta+\lambda_{\Pi\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\,,

(B.38a)

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}^{\langle{\mu\nu}\rangle}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi^{\mu\nu}}{\tau_{\pi}}-\beta_{\pi}\sigma^{\mu\nu}+2\pi^{\lambda\langle\mu}\omega^{\nu\rangle}_{\lambda}-\tau_{\pi\pi}\pi^{\lambda\langle\mu}\sigma^{\nu\rangle}_{\lambda}-\delta_{\pi\pi}\pi^{\mu\nu}\theta+\lambda_{\pi\Pi}\Pi\sigma^{\mu\nu}\,.

(B.38b)

Here
\omega^{\mu\nu}=\Delta^{\mu}_{\alpha}\Delta^{\nu}_{\beta}\partial_{[\beta}u_{\alpha]}
is the vorticity tensor. The relaxation times
\tau_{\pi}
and
\tau_{\Pi}
are given by Eq. (
3.57
). The remaining transport coefficients in quasiparticle viscous hydrodynamics (
VH
) are

\displaystyle\beta_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{I}_{32}}{T}\,,

(B.39a)

\displaystyle\beta_{\Pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{3}\beta_{\pi}-c_{s}^{2}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})+c_{s}^{2}m\frac{dm}{dT}\,\mathcal{I}_{11}\,,

(B.39b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\Pi\Pi}

\displaystyle=1-c_{s}^{2}-\frac{m^{4}}{9}(\bar{c}_{\mathcal{E}}\,\mathcal{I}_{00}{+}\bar{c}_{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{01})-m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})\Big(\bar{c}_{\mathcal{E}}\mathcal{I}_{21}{+}\frac{5}{3}\bar{c}_{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{22}{+}\frac{3}{m^{2}}\Big)\,,

(B.39c)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\Pi\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}-c_{s}^{2}+\frac{\bar{c}_{\pi}m^{2}\mathcal{I}_{22}}{3}\,,

(B.39d)

\displaystyle\tau_{\pi\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{10}{7}+\frac{4\,\bar{c}_{\pi}m^{2}\mathcal{I}_{22}}{7}\,,

(B.39e)

\displaystyle\delta_{\pi\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}+\frac{\bar{c}_{\pi}m^{2}\mathcal{I}_{22}}{3}-\bar{c}_{\pi}m\frac{dm}{d\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}})\mathcal{I}_{22}\,,

(B.39f)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pi\Pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{6}{5}-\frac{2m^{4}}{15}(\bar{c}_{\mathcal{E}}\,\mathcal{I}_{00}{+}\bar{c}_{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{01})\,.

(B.39g)

In standard viscous hydrodynamics (
VH
2), one performs an expansion in powers of
z=m/T\ll 1
and takes the fixed-mass limit
dm/dT\to 0
. The leading terms in the second-order transport coefficients are
[
70
]

\displaystyle\beta_{\pi}=\frac{\mathcal{E}{+}\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{eq}}}{5}+\mathcal{O}(z^{2})\,,

(B.40a)
